--- Page 2 ---
<meta>This is the title page of Carlo Lancillotti’s "Farmaceutica Mercuriale," published in Modena in 1683. It establishes the author’s credentials and the central debate of the book: the medicinal versus toxic nature of mercury.</meta>

# MERCURIAL
# PHARMACEUTICS
## OR THE
## TRIUMPH OF
# MERCURY

-> In which are described the most authoritative <note>original: "gravi," meaning weighty or influential</note> au- <-
-> thors who have written of it, and it is <-
-> made known to the world whether it be <-
-> poison or not, and whether it should be ac- <-
-> cepted for medicinal use <-
-> or rejected. <-

-> *A work of curiosity & great utility* <note>In the 17th century, a "curious" work was one that sought out rare knowledge or experimental truths</note> <-

### B Y
# CARLO LANCILLOTTI
## Medical Chemist, Citizen
## of Modena, and <term>Spagyricist</term> <note>A practitioner of Spagyrics, the alchemical method of preparing medicine by separating and then recombining the essential components of a substance</note> to
## <note>S.A.S. stands for *Sua Altezza Serenissima*</note> His Serene Highness of Modena

<image-desc>A decorative woodcut printer's ornament or vignette consisting of symmetrical, stylized floral and scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>

---

-> In Modena, by the Soliani Heirs, Ducal Printers. <-
-> With License from the Superiors <note>This indicates the text passed official censorship</note>, 1683. <-

<summary>The title page of Carlo Lancillotti's 1683 treatise on the medicinal uses of mercury, presenting it as a "triumph" and debating its safety. Lancillotti identifies himself as a chemist and spagyric practitioner under the patronage of the Duke of Modena.</summary>
<keywords>mercury, Carlo Lancillotti, Spagyrics, alchemy, pharmacology, Modena, 17th-century medicine, iatrochemistry</keywords>

# Errors occurred in the present book

Page 9, second to last line: for *dicitui*, read *dicitur* <note>Latin: "it is said"</note>.
Page 11, line 11: for *ha c*, read *lac* <note>Latin: "milk"</note>.
Page 12, line 17: for *Hemastis*, read *Hematitis* <note>Hematite, an iron-oxide mineral often discussed in early modern medicine for its perceived ability to stop bleeding</note>.
Page 16, line 24: for *fue, rit*, read *fuerit* <note>Latin: "it shall have been"</note>.
Page 20, line 9: for *affectiuum* <note>affective</note>, read *effectiuum* <note>effective</note>;
line 16: for *adere*, read *adæret* <note>Latin: "it adheres"</note>.
Page 24, line 23: for *Ex*, read *Et* <note>Latin: "And"</note>.
Page 26, line 3: for *Choleficum*, read *colericum* <note>Choleric; relating to yellow bile, one of the four bodily humors</note>.
Page 27, line 29: for *quelli* <note>those</note>, read *quello* <note>that</note>; line 30:
for *suno*, read *sano* <note>healthy</note>.
Page 33, line 15: for *densum* <note>neuter</note>, read *densam* <note>feminine; adjusting the Latin gender of the word "dense"</note>.
Page 34, line 15: for *eorumpi*, read *corumpi* <note>Latin: "to be corrupted" or "to spoil"</note>.
Page 36, line 28: for *tuum* <note>your</note>, read *suum* <note>his or its</note>.
Page 38, <margin>in the margin</margin>: for *si suis*, read *si scis* <note>Latin: "if you know"</note>.
Page 40, line 2: for *qued*, read *quod* <note>Latin: "that" or "which"</note>.
Page 42, line 27: for *nostræ*, read *noster* <note>Latin: "our"</note>; line 31:
for *ex*, read *&* <note>The ampersand symbol for "and"</note>.
Page 54, line 4: for *essaminaua* <note>was examining</note>, read *essamina* <note>examines</note>.
Page 57, line 2: for *Ardonimus*, read *Ardouinus* <note>Likely referring to Sante Ardoini, a famous 15th-century physician and author of a treatise on poisons</note>;
line 15: and furthermore <note>original: "& di piu"</note>.
Page 62, line 29, for *ad molum*, read *ad motum* <note>Latin: "by motion"</note>;
line 30: for *egitatum*, read *agitatum* <note>Latin: "shaken" or "agitated"</note>; line 31: for *bu-
llierido exilio*, read *bulliendo exilit* <note>Latin: "it leaps forth by boiling"</note>, and in
<margin>the margin</margin>: for *dixis*, read *dixit* <note>Latin: "he said"</note>.
Page 64, line 7: for *& Arist 2*, read *from Arist. 4* <note>Reference to Aristotle's works</note>; line
10: for *habetur* <note>it is held</note>, read *habere* <note>to have</note>.
Page 71, line 6: for the hands <note>original: "le mani"</note>, read the Sea <note>original: "il Mare"</note>.
Page 81, line 22: for there find <note>original: "colà troua"</note>, read Calatrava <note>Referring to the Order of Calatrava or a specific Spanish geographical reference</note>.

# Errors found in the present book

Page 9, second to last line: for *dicitui*, read *dicitur* <note>Latin: "it is said".</note>
Page 11, line 11: for *ha c*, read *hac* <note>Latin: "this".</note>

Page 16, line 24: for *fue, rit*, read *fuerit* <note>Latin: "it will have been".</note>
Page 20, line 9: for *affectiuum*, read *effectiuum* <note>A correction from "affective" to "effective".</note>
line 16: for *adere*, read *adæret* <note>Latin: "it adheres".</note>
Page 24, line 23: for *Ex*, read *And* <note>original: "Et"</note>
Page 26, line 3: for *Choleficum*, read *choleric* <note>original: "colericum", referring to the "yellow bile" humor.</note>
Page 27, line 29: for *those*, read *that* <note>original Italian: "quelli" to "quello"</note> line 30: for *funo*, read *fano* <note>Italian: likely "they do" (fanno).</note>
Page 33, line 15: for *densum*, read *densam* <note>A Latin grammatical correction of gender.</note>
Page 34, line 15: for *eorumpi*, read *corumpi* <note>Latin: "to be corrupted" or "to spoil".</note>
Page 36, line 28: for *fuum*, read *suum* <note>Latin: "his own".</note>
Page 38, <margin>for *si suis*, read *si scis*</margin> <note>A change from "if his" to "if you know".</note>
Page 40, line 2: for *qued*, read *quod* <note>Latin: "which/that".</note>
Page 42, line 27: for *nostræ*, read *noster* <note>Latin: "our".</note> line 31: for *ex*, read *&* <note>The symbol & denotes "and".</note>
Page 54, line 4: for *he examined*, read *he examines* <note>original Italian: "essaminaua" to "essamina"</note>
Page 57, line 2: for *Ardonimus*, read *Ardouinus* <note>Refers to Sante Ardoini, a 15th-century physician famous for his work on poisons.</note>
line 15: and more <note>original Italian: "& di piu"</note>
Page 62, line 29: for *ad molum*, read *ad motum* <note>Latin: "to the motion".</note>
line 30: for *egitatum*, read *agitatum* <note>Latin: "agitated" or "shaken".</note> line 31: for *bulierido exilio*, read *bulliendo exilit* <note>Latin: "it leaps out while boiling".</note>
& <margin>for *dixis*, read *dixit*</margin> <note>Latin: "he said".</note>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Errata Corrige" or list of corrections from the previous page, addressing typographical and spelling errors found in the text, focusing particularly on alchemical and botanical terminology.</meta>

Page 88, last line: for *then*, read *then* <note>original Latin: "tum" to "tunc". Both mean "then," but the author prefers the latter for this specific context.</note>
Page 98, line 26: for *threefold*, read *three times as much* <note>original: "tretanto" to "tre volte tanto".</note>
Page 102, line 9: for *corobando*, read *coobando* <note>Refers to cohobation, an alchemical process of redistilling a liquid over its own solid residue to concentrate its properties.</note>
Page 111, line 18: for *salt*, read *sun* <note>original: "sale" to "sole". The letters 'a' and 'o' were frequently confused in early printing.</note>
Page 123, line 9: for *corobi*, read *coobi* <note>A correction related to the technical term for cohobation.</note>
Page 129, line 8: for *may enjoy*, read *enjoys* <note>original Italian: "goda" to "gode".</note>
Page 139, line 22: for *rectify*, read *revivify* <note>original: "rettificare" to "reuiuifficare" (revivificare). In chemistry and alchemy, to revivify meant to restore a substance—such as mercury—to its natural state.</note>
Page 140, line 18: for *ravenatum qua scatebati*, read *which gushed with poison* <note>original Latin: "venenatam que scatebat".</note>
Page 147, line 1: for *inorando*, read *sprinkling* <note>original: "irorando", meaning to moisten or bedew a substance.</note>
Page 153, line 16: for *a little*, read *little by little* <note>original: "à poco" to "à poco à poco".</note>
Page 164, line 15: for *infected*, read *infected* <note>original: "infetton" to "infetto".</note> line 17: for *infected*, read *infected* <note>original: "infetatto" to "infetto".</note>
Page 166, line 15: for *laoi*, read *washed* <note>original: "laui" (lavi), meaning to wash or rinse.</note>
Page 175, line 24: for *corruption*, read *corrosion* <note>original: "corottione" to "corossione". A critical distinction in alchemical processes involving acids.</note>
Page 183, lines 30 and 31: for *corbando*, read *coobando* <note>Another correction regarding cohobation.</note>
Page 193, line 10: for *corbi*, read *coobi* <note>Another correction regarding cohobation.</note>
Page 202, line 17: for *Btellio*, read *Bdellium* <note>original: "Bdelio". Bdellium is a semi-transparent oleo-gum resin used in ancient and early modern medicine.</note>
Page 205, line 10: for *peufito*, read *itching* <note>original: "prurito".</note> line 15: for *enela*, read *elecampane* <note>original: "enulla". Elecampane (Inula helenium) is a medicinal plant used for respiratory and digestive ailments.</note>

On the <term>sublimation</term> <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then allowing it to solidify again, used to purify chemicals like mercury.</note> of Mercury: first common sublimate, chapter 1, page 135
Second sublimate, chapter 2, page 136
Third sublimate, chapter 3, page 136

Fifth Philosophical sublimate, or the <term>Metallic Vinegar</term> <note>A corrosive or solvent-like substance derived from metals, often used as a primary agent in alchemical transformations.</note> of the Philosophers, chapter 5, page 138
Sixth common sweet sublimate: sweet Mercury, or the <term>Mitigated Dragon</term> <note>A poetic alchemical name for calomel (mercurous chloride); the "dragon" represents the volatile mercury, which has been "mitigated" or made less corrosive and dangerous.</note>, chapter 6, page 144
Seventh sweet, red sublimate, or the <term>Coralline Arcanum</term> <note>A red mercuric oxide preparation named for its coral-like color; an "arcanum" refers to a secret or powerful medicinal formula.</note> of <term>Paracelsus</term> <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), known as Paracelsus, was a revolutionary Swiss physician who championed the use of chemicals in medicine.</note>, chapter 7, page 146
Eighth sweetened sublimate, or the <term>Quinta Essentia</term> <note>The "Fifth Essence" or quintessence—the most pure and concentrated medicinal part of a substance.</note> of sublimated Mercury, chapter 8, page 148
Ninth <term>Lunar</term> <note>In alchemy, "Lunar" refers to silver, the metal associated with the Moon.</note> sublimate, chapter 9, page 150
Tenth Lunar sublimate, chapter 10, page 150
Eleventh sublimate, <term>Metallic Laudanum</term> <note>While modern laudanum is opium-based, alchemical "laudanum" (from the Latin *laudare*, to praise) often referred to highly esteemed mineral medicines, sometimes containing gold or mercury.</note>, or Mercurial [Laudanum], chapter 11, page 151
Twelfth sweet sublimate, or the <term>Panacea</term> <note>A universal remedy believed to cure all diseases.</note> of purged Mercury, chapter 12, page 152

Purgations of <term>diaphoretic</term> <note>A substance used to induce sweating, which was thought to expel toxins or "humors" from the body.</note> cinnabar, chapter 14, page 154

Another diaphoretic cinnabar, chapter 16, page 156
Most beautiful preparation of purging and diaphoretic Mercury, chapter 17, page 157

# TRIUMPH

->TO THE READER:<-

Although I have discussed his brother <note>original: "Germano." In the context of early modern chemistry and alchemy, Mercury and Antimony were often described as "brothers" or closely related substances.</note> Mercury intermittently within the *Triumph of Antimony*, nevertheless it would seem a very great injustice to him if I did not let him triumph specifically. My goal is to make known to the World the heroic virtues of this Hero, for he alone is sufficient to complete—or, to put it better, to supply—an entire <term>Medical Dispensary</term> <note>original: "Officina Medicinale." This refers to a complete pharmacy or laboratory capable of producing all necessary medicines.</note> to cure every kind of disease. This is despite the fact that many ignorant people slander <note>original: "sbiāfmano," likely an archaic variant or printing error for "biasimano," meaning to criticize or find fault with.</note> him and seek with all their power to strip him of his reputation, and they do not realize...

no, for they do wrong, because as it is the most vital enemy of the <term>French Disease</term> <note>original: "morbo Gallico," the common historical name for syphilis, which was famously treated with mercury.</note>, they might one day have need of it. It is indeed true, and I warn them, that if it is not prepared by a skilled practitioner, it plays nasty tricks; just as if it is not administered as it should be, both internally and externally—

<margin>Purgative medicines should only be used by their master,</margin>
—therefore, he who does not know it should leave it be, and not trifle with it, because he will be the one trifled with. —and in a very short time. This was not because that dose of Gamboge was capable of killing a man as robust as he was, but rather because it was poorly presented, ignorantly administered, and given at an inappropriate time. For the person who gave it to him was an ignoramus who did not know that during the <term>Dog Days</term> <note>original: "giorni Caniculari," the hottest weeks of summer (July and August), historically believed to be a dangerous time for surgery or strong purgatives due to the influence of the Dog Star, Sirius.</note>, as it was then, one does not give any other

<margin>Medicines must be given at the opportune time.</margin>
medicine, except in cases of extreme necessity; and even then, as much as possible, one seeks to give light ones, rather than giving a violent medicine that is extremely "hot" <note>In the humoral theory of the time, medicines were classified by qualities like hot, cold, moist, or dry.</note> during times when nature finds itself debilitated and has more need of being strengthened than weakened by any violence. Thus, if this man had stuck to practicing his own trade, which was that of a <term>Tinsmith</term> <note>original: "Stagnaro," a craftsman who makes or repairs tin vessels.</note>—that is, one of those who make tin vessels—he would not have fallen into such an excess. <summary>The author warns against unqualified people practicing medicine based on limited reading, noting that even good medicines become dangerous when dispensed without regard for a patient's age, sex, or constitution. # BOOK ONE
### O F
# MERCURY
### *AND ITS DEFINITION*
## According to the Ancient Greek Physicians.

->*Chapter 1.*<-

<margin>The Greeks, as can be seen; Dioscorides.</margin>
**Mercury**, called **Quicksilver** <note>original: "Argento viuo." Historically, the name "quicksilver" comes from the archaic sense of "quick," meaning "living"—a reference to the metal's unique liquid state and fluid movement.</note> by the common people; as far as can be judged from their writings, it was very little known by the ancient **Physicians**. I wish to set these writings forth here in the same form that I have found them, and first to begin with **Dioscorides** <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD) was a Greek physician and pharmacologist whose work *De Materia Medica* was the primary medical text for over 1,500 years.</note>, who writes these precise words about it in book five, Chapter 60: *“Quicksilver is made from minium <note>In antiquity, "minium" referred to cinnabar (mercury sulfide), though today the term is used for red lead. Dioscorides is clarifying a common confusion in nomenclature.</note>, which is falsely called Cinnabar, in an earthen pan [with] an iron...”* <note>original: "Argentum viuum, fit ex minio, quod falso Cinabris dicitur in, fictilem pattinam feream cun-"</note>

# BOOK ONE
### O F
# MERCURY
### AND ITS DEFINITION
### According to the Ancient Greek Physicians.

->Chapter 1.<-

<margin>The Greeks, as can be seen; and Dioscorides.</margin>
**Mercury**, commonly called **quicksilver** <note>original: "Argento viuo." This name, meaning "living silver," reflects the metal's unique liquid state and rapid movement.</note> by the common people; from what can be judged, it was very little known to the **Ancient Physicians** based on their writings. I wish to set these down here in the same form that I have found them, beginning first with **Dioscorides**, <note>Pedanius Dioscorides (c. 40–90 AD) was a Greek physician and botanist whose "De Materia Medica" was the foundational text for European medicine and pharmacy for centuries.</note> who writes about it in the fifth book, Chapter 60, with these precise words: *Quicksilver is made from minium <term>Minium: In antiquity, this term was often used for cinnabar (mercuric sulfide), the primary ore of mercury, though it later came to mean red lead.</term> (which is falsely called Cinnabar) in an earthen pan with an iron lid...* <note>original Latin: "Argentum viuum, fit ex minio, quod falso Cinabris dicitur in, fictilem pattinam feream cunctam." The author is quoting a Latin translation of Dioscorides' Greek text, describing the distillation/sublimation process used to extract mercury from its ore.</note>

<summary>This page opens the first book of the treatise, defining Mercury (quicksilver) through the lens of ancient Greek medical authorities, specifically quoting Dioscorides' description of its extraction from ore.</summary>
<keywords>Mercury, quicksilver, Dioscorides, Ancient Physicians, cinnabar, minium, history of medicine</keywords>

For the soot that adheres to the cup, when scraped off, is **Quicksilver**. <note>original: "Argentum Viuum." This describes a basic sublimation process where the mercury vaporizes and then condenses on the cooler lid.</note> Furthermore, it is found in the roofs of silver mines gathered in drops. It is stored in lead, tin, or glass vessels, for it eats away at other materials. <note>Mercury forms amalgams with many metals, literally "dissolving" them, which is why the author notes it "eats" other materials.</note> And this is what so famous a man has to say regarding the origin and birth of Mercury; as for its virtues and qualities, here is what he thinks in the sixth book, Chapter 28: *Quicksilver, if drunk, produces the same effects as the froth of silver* <term>Froth of silver: Also known as litharge (lead oxide), a byproduct of refining silver that was known to be poisonous.</term> *namely griping pains and intestinal suffering; and the same remedies must be used. It seems, however, to be beneficial when drunk even in large quantities; but it must afterwards be vomited up.* <note>Ancient physicians sometimes prescribed drinking mercury to physically clear intestinal blockages through its sheer weight, though they were wary of its toxicity.</note>

**Paul of Aegina** <note>A 7th-century Byzantine Greek physician whose medical encyclopedia summarized much of the ancient world's knowledge.</note> in Book 5, Chapter 64, page 158, appears to follow the words of Dioscorides when describing the properties of Quicksilver, saying: *And Quicksilver, when drunk, brings about the same effects as the froth of silver* <note>original Latin: "spuma Argenti." ## Definition of Mercury, its Origin, and Qualities according to the Ancient Arabic Physicians.

->Chapter II.<-

Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose "Canon of Medicine" remained a standard medical authority in Europe and the Islamic world for centuries.</note> and the other Arabs who wrote after the aforementioned Greeks laugh at what the said Greeks claim—namely, that mercury is artificial. This is because they had knowledge of <term>Cinnabar</term> <note>The primary ore of mercury (mercury sulfide), found naturally as a red mineral; it was often confused with other red substances in antiquity.</note> that is used in the workshops, as can be seen by what he says in his works, writing of it in the form of a question in Book Two, Treatise Two, Chapter 47, with these precise words: *What is Quicksilver?* <note>original Latin: "Argentum Vivum quid est?"</note>

<margin>Avicenna.</margin>
> *One type of quicksilver is that which is purified from its own ore, and another is that which is extracted from the stones of its ore.* <note>original Latin: "Argentum viuum aliud est purgatum à Minera sua, & aliud extrahitur ex lapidibus Mineræ suæ"</note>

<summary>This page marks a transition from Greek alchemical traditions to the Arabic medical tradition, specifically citing Avicenna to refute the Greek idea that mercury is a man-made substance rather than a natural mineral extract.</summary>
<keywords>Avicenna, mercury, quicksilver, cinnabar, Arabic medicine, alchemy, chrysopoeia, mineralogy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument regarding the natural versus artificial origin of mercury. It specifically addresses the views of Galen, which the author (citing Avicenna) seeks to correct, and begins a technical definition of mercury's elemental composition.</meta>

...with its fire. Just as gold and silver <note>original Latin: "Aurum, & Argentum"</note> are made. He further adds: "And Galen <note>A prominent Greek physician and philosopher (c. 129–216 AD) whose theories dominated Western medicine for centuries.</note> and others indeed believed that it [mercury] was made by art, like <term>Litharge</term> <note>A secondary mineral form of lead monoxide, often produced during the refining of silver from lead ores.</note>; because it is extracted with fire, and because its substance is similar to <term>Uzifur</term> <note>From the Arabic 'al-zanjifur', referring to cinnabar or the red pigment vermilion.</note>. He therefore thought that it is nothing other than [mercury] from <term>Algenzipher</term> <note>Another transliteration of the Arabic term for cinnabar.</note> in a luted vessel <note>A vessel sealed with 'lute' (a clay or paste) to make it airtight for chemical reactions.</note>, over which a fire is lit, and it is sublimated; but it is not so: rather Uzifur (that is, <term>Cinnabar</term> <note>The primary ore of mercury, mercury(II) sulfide.</note>) is made from it with Sulfur; then it is possible that the substance of Quicksilver <note>original Latin: "Argenti Viui"</note> be extracted from it."

And in a treatise that he wrote on minerals, which is found in a book titled *The Art of Gold-Making* <note>original Latin: "Ars Aurifera," a famous collection of alchemical texts published in the late 16th century.</note>, Volume 1, page 243, in its third chapter, he writes thus: <margin>Avicenna in the book titled *The Art of Gold-Making*</margin> *Quicksilver is like water which is mixed with an extremely subtle earth in a strong sulfurous mixture, until it does not rest upon a flat surface; and this is due to the great dryness that it possesses, and therefore it does not adhere to what it touches. And its whiteness comes from the clarity of that water, and from the whiteness of the subtle earth that is within it. Its property is that it is coagulated by the vapor of sulfur, and for-* <note>The text cuts off mid-word (likely 'formatur' or 'fortiter') at the end of the page.</note>

<summary>This page refutes Galen's theory that mercury is an artificial byproduct, explaining instead its relationship to cinnabar and providing Avicenna's alchemical definition of mercury as a combination of "clear water" and "subtle earth."</summary>

...until it is solidified <note>The word "te" completes the verb "congelante" or "congelatur" from the previous page.</note> by lead or easily by the vapor of sulfur. It appears, moreover, that **Quicksilver** <note>original Latin: "Argentum viuum," now commonly known as mercury.</note>, and those things which are similar to it, is the element of all <term>liquefiable substances</term> <note>original Latin: "liquabiliū," referring to metals and minerals that can be melted or turned into a liquid state.</note>; this is either because all liquefiable things are converted back to it when they are melted, or because they do not melt before they are heated, and once they have been melted, they appear red. 

But **Lead** <note>original Latin: "Plumbum"</note> without a doubt <note>The OCR "procul ubi ū" is likely a transcription of the Latin "procul dubio," meaning "without a doubt."</note> is Quicksilver when it is melted, yet it does not melt unless it is heated; and when it has been melted, it turns into a liquid common to all liquefiable things, namely a fiery redness. For this reason, Quicksilver is mixed with these bodies, because it is part of their very substance. However, these bodies differ in their composition from it, just as Quicksilver differs from things similar to itself, and the mixtures that are blended with them until they are frozen <note>In an alchemical context, "frozen" or "congealed" refers to a liquid metal becoming solid.</note>.

But if it should be, namely, a pure and excellent sulfur with a clear redness, and there should be in it the power of fiery... <meta>catchword: ignei (fiery)</meta>

...of a simple, non-burning fieriness <note>original Latin: "igneitatis simplicis non urentis"</note>, it will be the best thing that **Alchemists** <note>original Latin: "Alchimistæ"</note> can find, so that they may make **Gold** <note>original Latin: "Aurum"</note> from it. And if the **Quicksilver** <note>original Latin: "Argentum vivum"</note> were of good substance and the **Sulfur** <note>original Latin: "Sulphur"</note> were not pure—meaning that there is a burning force within it—it will convert it into **Copper** <note>original Latin: "es," referring to "aes," a term for copper or bronze</note>. However, if the Quicksilver were poor, impure, and earthy, and if the sulfur with it is unclean, **Iron** <note>original Latin: "ferrum"</note> is made from it. **Tin** <note>original Latin: "Stahnum"</note>, truly, seems to have good Quicksilver but bad sulfur, and these are not well ground together, but joined as if by small components, and therefore it does not become such <note>meaning it does not achieve the perfection of gold or silver</note>. Of coarse **Lead** <note>original Latin: "Plumbi"</note>, the Quicksilver is bad, heavy, and yellowish, and its Sulfur is bad, possessing an evil, stinking, and weak vapor; hence it does not solidify <note>original Latin: "gelatur," literally "to freeze," used in alchemy to mean the transition from liquid to solid metal</note> well.

<margin>Avicenna in his aforementioned book</margin>

And in another treatise in the aforementioned book on page 260, which he wrote concerning the **Philosopher’s Stone** <note>original Latin: "Philosophici lapis"</note>, in Chapter 1, he says these exact words about Quicksilver: "Quicksilver is cold and moist, and God created all minerals from it or with it; and it is airy and flees from fire. But when it has remained in the fire for a short time, it performs marvelous and strange works."

...of a very subtle substance, having weight from terrestrial water and a downward motion, possessing clarity, fluidity, and a **silvery color**. Indeed, **Quicksilver** <note>original Latin: "Argentum vivum"</note> clearly appears to have a dense substance, like the **unicorn** <note>original Latin: "monoceros"; in alchemy, the unicorn often symbolizes the spirit of mercury or a singular, untamable purity</note>, because of the heaviness of its immense weight: for it outweighs **gold** <note>original Latin: "aurum"</note> while it is in its own nature, and it is of the strongest composition and uniform nature: since it is not separated from itself into parts, nor does it allow itself to be divided in any way. This is because it either withdraws from the fire with its whole substance <note>referring to mercury's tendency to evaporate entirely when heated</note> or remains in the fire.

But when its parts are thickened <note>original Latin: "inspissantur," referring to the thickening or coagulation of the liquid</note> by fire, it no longer allows itself to be corrupted, nor does it permit itself to be raised further <note>meaning it no longer evaporates or "ascends" as a vapor</note> by the entry of the raging flame.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion from page 28 regarding how the purity and qualities of sulfur and quicksilver (mercury) determine which metal is formed. pure, clean, and of the best quality, clear with a reddish tint, and containing the fiery power of sulfur without its burning property; it will be the finest material that those who practice alchemy can take up, and from it they make the Sun <term>Sun: In the symbolic language of alchemy, *Sol* or the Sun refers to Gold.</term>.

For if the quicksilver <note>original Latin: *Argentum Vivum*</note> is of good substance, but the sulfur is impure and burning, it converts that quicksilver into copper <note>original Latin: *Es*</note>. Furthermore, if the quicksilver is stony, unclean, and earthy, and the sulfur is not pure, iron will be made from it.

Tin <note>original Latin: *Stannum*</note>, however, seems to possess good and pure quicksilver, but the sulfur is bad and not well-mixed. Lead <note>original Latin: *Plumbum*</note> indeed has a coarse, bad, heavy, and yellowish quicksilver; its sulfur is also bad, of poor taste, foul-smelling, and of weak virtue <note>In this context, "virtue" refers to the inherent medicinal or chemical potency of a substance.</note>; hence it does not congeal well.

And these are the very same words spoken in the aforementioned text by the philosopher Avicenna <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose works on medicine and alchemy were foundational to European science.</note>; and similarly, Richard the Englishman <note>Richardus Anglicus, a 13th-century alchemical author known for his work *Correctorium Alchimiae*.</note> says the following in the third chapter of his *Correctorium*, citing Aristotle:

<margin>Richard the Englishman.</margin>
The nature of all liquefiable things <note>referring to metals that can be melted</note> is from quicksilver, and its substance is such because it is congealed by vapor or heat through that same quicksilver.

<summary>This page details how different grades of sulfur and mercury combine to form specific metals (gold, copper, iron, tin, and lead) according to Avicenna and Richard the Englishman.</summary>

If, however, the Sulphur is not fixed, it hinders proper fusion by either happening too early <note>anticipating the reaction before the other elements are ready</note> or by being corrupted by the fire.

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>The previous page concluded with the Aristotelian principle that "the corruption of one is the generation of another," referring to the breakdown of metals. This page continues that thought by explaining the specific color transitions (black, white, yellow, red) that signal these transformations.</meta>

**Citrination** <term>citrination: (original: *citrinatio*) the third of the four major stages of the alchemical process, characterized by a yellowing of the matter.</term> is nothing other than complete digestion: nor is **whiteness** <term>whiteness: (original: *albedo*) the stage following the "blackness" where the matter is purified.</term> anything other than the removal of **blackness** <term>blackness: (original: *nigredo*) the initial stage of "death" or decomposition of the matter.</term>. For heat acting upon the moist produces blackness first; and acting upon the dry, it works whiteness, and upon the white, it produces the yellow color.

And this indeed can be observed in the **calcination** <term>calcination: the reduction of a metal to a powder or ash through intense heat.</term> of **Lead**, which is converted first into black ash; afterwards into white; and then into an intense yellow, or red. Thus **white** or **red Sulphur** is obtained from a single matter of metals that has been fully purified, though digested and cooked in a different manner.

Therefore the **Philosopher** <note>likely referring to Aristotle or Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary father of alchemy.</note> says that in all **Silver** there is **White Sulphur**, just as in all **Gold** there is **Red Sulphur**. But such Sulphur is not found upon the earth; rather, as is said of **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose chemical theories were central to medieval alchemy.</note>, it exists within these two bodies. Therefore, let us subtly prepare these two bodies, so that we may obtain the **Sulphur** and **Quicksilver** from that same matter out of which Gold and Silver were originally formed beneath the earth.

For these are the shining bodies in which the "tinting rays" <note>original: *radij tingentes*; the transformative power believed to stain or "tinge" base metals into noble ones</note> reside, which color other bodies with true whiteness and redness according to how they themselves were prepared. For through

<margin>
Adept, note these words, mark these mysteries; here lies what you seek. If you know how to choose, you have found the **Secret of Secrets** <note>original: *Arcan Arcanorum*</note> hidden within these words.
</margin>

<meta>This page continues the specific technical discourse attributed to Raymond Lully regarding the internal composition of Mercury (Quicksilver). To better understand <note>original: "Ad meglius intelligendum"</note> the difference between **sulfur** <term>Sulfur: In alchemical theory, Sulfur represents the active, masculine principle of combustion and "soul," though here it refers to a specific internal component of metals.</term> and **quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: Also known as Mercury; the passive, feminine principle representing fluidity, "spirit," and the metallic essence.</term> through their separate substances with respect to their compositions, we wish to inform you that **quicksilver** contains its own proper **hot and sharp sulfur**; and likewise, **sulfur** contains its own **quicksilver**, which is like air and the **food of fire** <note>original: "epulum ignis" — a poetic way of saying it sustains the flame.</note>.

This is because the said quicksilver is, in its own proper root, first composed through the ingenuity of nature from a white, subtle, sulfurous earth—both hot and airy—mixed and gathered in clear water, until it has become a single **homogeneous substance** in its metallic nature. This occurs with a measure of true and clear elementary proportion, aided by the **stellar influence** <term>Stellar influence: The Neoplatonic and alchemical belief that celestial bodies (stars and planets) radiated "virtue" or formative power that directed the growth of minerals underground.</term>, which is the virtue of the metallic form through the great concordance of the said matter. 

This influence acts as a divine and worthy cause through which the individuality of **quicksilver** is completed, and in which the form of metallic nature is first established in such a way that the **dry** is tempered by the **moist**, and the **moist** by the **dry**, without any separation of one from the other. <meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the internal composition of metals, specifically addressing the confusion between "common" substances and their "philosophical" counterparts. It transitions from the "dry" qualities of the previous page to the roles of water and earth as separate substances.</meta>

...with the two separate substances, namely of water and earth, through which it is granted to you to understand that when we speak of **Sulfur** or **Quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: Also known as mercury, the principle of fluidity and spirit in alchemy.</term>, the entire intellect is troubled because the whole is of a single composition. 

But when we speak of **exterminable sulfur** <note>original: "sulphurexterminabile" — this refers to the impure, combustible part of sulfur that is "exterminated" or purged during the alchemical process to reach the pure "fixed" sulfur.</note>, understand this by the property of the terrestrial element. But when we say **common quicksilver**, understand the **water of its vivacity** (extracted from **Vitriol** <term>Vitriol: A glassy mineral (sulfate) used to create powerful acidic solvents.</term> and from **Saltpeter** <term>Saltpeter: Potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in "strong waters" or acids used to dissolve metals.</term>). This is according to the virtue and reason that its subject requires through extremes and means, with substances separated through **natural sublimation** <term>Sublimation: The process of heating a substance until it turns to vapor and سپس reverts to solid form, used here to describe a spiritual or physical refinement.</term> which is concordant and harmonizes with nature.

Although **Sulfur** is present in all elements, it is nevertheless never "exterminable" <note>In this context, the author means the essential, inner sulfur is indestructible, unlike the outer, impure crust.</note>, for it is nothing but the vapor and action of air and fire. # Whether Mercury be a Poison, and in what its malignancy consists, and whether it can be safely used.

### Chapter IV.

It already seems to me that I have described enough in the first chapter of this volume regarding the malignancy attributed by the Ancients to **Mercury** <term>Mercury: often called quicksilver, this liquid metal was a source of great debate in early medicine; while ancient authorities feared it as a toxin, later "iatrochemists" sought to harness it as a potent cure.</term>, and how it was placed by them among the number of poisons. This is also done by **Vueikardi** <note>Referring to Johann Jacob Wecker (1528–1586), a Swiss physician and author of highly influential medical compendiums.</note> in his book titled *Pharmaceutical Treasury, Book Five, Chapter 7, On the Poisons of Metals, page 66* <note>original Latin: "Tesaurus Pharmaceuticus lib. quinto cap. 7. de Venenis Mettallorum car. 66."</note>, speaking thus of **Quicksilver** <note>original Latin: "Argentum viuum"</note>:

> "If this be drunk too liberally, it kills by an excess of cold and moisture; for it putrefies the innate humor of the heart and thereafter congeals the **Blood**, causing the vital spirits to be extinguished, as has been discovered by the experience of physicians, let it be read." <note>original Latin: "Hoc si largius bibatur, excessu summe frigido, & frigido, & humido interimit putrefacit enim innatum cordis humorem ac subinde congellat, Sanguinem, vitales Spritus extingui, quod experientia medicis compertum est legatur"</note>

This story is false; I have never seen it.

This is the story of a certain **Selapharios** <note>original: "Selapharios"</note> who, while seized by a fever, drank **Quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: also known as Argentum Vivum or Mercury; a liquid metal that fascinated and terrified early modern physicians.</term> in place of water, and a few hours later he died frozen <note>original Latin: "congellatus"; in the medical theory of the time, mercury was considered "cold" and "moist," and an overdose was thought to extinguish the body's vital heat.</note>. See also **Mattioli** <note>Pietro Andrea Mattioli (1501–1577), an influential Italian physician and herbalist.</note> on page 999. It is, however, especially hostile to the membranous parts, such as the membranes of the skull and the nerves—hence, if it is handled for too long in the palm of the hands, it induces nausea and vomiting. 

Secondly, it is harmful in the way that many **goldsmiths** <note>Goldsmiths used mercury in a process called "fire-gilding," where a gold-mercury amalgam was heated, releasing toxic mercury vapors.</note> are injured by it, and are sometimes suddenly killed unless they protect themselves. Yet, clearly, there are some who believe that **Quicksilver** <note>original Latin: "Argentum vivum"</note> can be administered internally without any harm. They are persuaded by the argument that, by its own weight, it exerts a force such that it moves through the body like a hook through a grape <note>original Latin: "ut in acine hamo inambulat"; a metaphor for the metal's ability to push through obstructions by sheer density.</note>, always seeking an exit until it is ejected again through the bowels. 

This can be read in **Paré** <note>Ambroise Paré (c. 1510–1590), the pioneering French royal surgeon.</note> in his *Book on Poisons, Chapter 38*, where he firmly affirms that it is the highest and final remedy for difficulties in childbirth and for killing intestinal worms. But truly, while we may wonder at this, we must fear that its poisonous force always constricts the stomach and intestines; indeed, it leads a person to **epilepsy** and **apoplexy**. See **The Conciliator** <note>Pietro d'Abano (c. 1257–1316), a famous medieval scholar who sought to "reconcile" the differences between Greek and Arabic medical traditions.</note>, Chapter 5, *On the Remedies for Poisons*; **Avicenna** <note>Ibn Sina (c. 980–1037), whose medical encyclopedia was the standard text in Europe for centuries.</note>, Book 4, Volume 1, Chapter 2; and **Paul of Aegina** <note>A 7th-century Byzantine Greek physician known for his surgical compendiums.</note>.

<margin>
It is to be understood that merely by handling it with the hands, even if it be for a long time, Mercury possesses an emetic power <term>emetic power: the ability to induce vomiting.</term>. For women in labor, a weight of a scruple <term>scruple: a small unit of apothecary weight, roughly 1.3 grams.</term> is given; to children, it is administered in a quantity the size of millet seeds.

These, then, are the harmful qualities of **Mercury**, which consist—from what can be judged and as daily experience reveals—more from an accidental cause than from a natural one, and furthermore, more from quantity than from quality (if it is even true that it possesses any inherent malice at all).

All these processes of **sublimation** <term>Sublimation: the process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then collecting it as it cools back into a solid form.</term>, **precipitation** <term>Precipitation: the process of causing a substance to settle out of a liquid solution.</term>, and **calcination** <term>Calcination: heating a metal or mineral to high temperatures to remove volatile substances or to oxidize it, usually turning it into a powder.</term> have the same effect on it as a mask has on a man's face: in appearance, it makes him seem like someone else, but once it is removed, he is exactly who he was before. **Fourth:** Why does **Gold** not float upon it, as we ourselves have also experienced? **Pliny** <note>Pliny the Elder, author of the encyclopedic *Natural History*.</note> answers that this is because **Quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: The common name for liquid Mercury, from the Latin *argentum vivum*.</term> corrodes Gold, and thus hides it, and the Gold embraces it; by embracing and surrounding it, it becomes one and incorporates itself with the Gold, perhaps due to the greatest **Sympathy**. But a better answer might be that Gold, among all metals and mixtures, is more heavy because of the density and lack of pores in its substance; and therefore it seeks the bottom of the Quicksilver even in very unequal proportions. For in two pounds of Quicksilver, six ounces of **Lead** float, yet even a **drachma** <term>Drachma: A small unit of weight derived from ancient Greek currency, commonly used in apothecary and alchemical measures.</term> of gold seeks the bottom, which is for no other reason than that Gold is of a most dense and non-porous substance.

**Fifth:** Why does Quicksilver penetrate vessels of gold, silver, copper, iron, or other metals? Did Pliny say correctly in Book 33, Chapter 6, that it is the **Poison** <note>original: *venenum*</note> of all metals—or as another manuscript might read, the **Poison** of all things?

Does this happen because of its thinness and marvelous fluidity, which is also evident in the parts of the elements that always desire to be united to the whole? And so **Mercury** <term>Mercury: Known as "quicksilver," this liquid metal was a central focus of alchemy due to its ability to change states.</term> seems to perpetuate itself, showing itself to be incorruptible. For through this, the **resurrection of the Dead** can be almost naturally proven, even though this is a divine work and above nature; for since quicksilver is otherwise a very heavy body, it is immediately dissolved into a very light thing, namely smoke, with which it ascends; and immediately that same smoke, which is otherwise very light, is immediately resolved back into Mercury, a very heavy thing.

This transmutation of that marvelous body is a wondrous thing, which, moved by fire, and turned and agitated by fire as far as the lid [of the vessel], "boiling by leaping out," as **Olympiodorus** <note>Olympiodorus the Younger (c. 495–570 AD), an Alexandrine philosopher and alchemist known for his commentaries on chemical transformation.</note> says, it both effervesces and [returns] to its kindred...

Seventh: why is it not mixed with liquids, but with harder substances like metals? Is it because of its extreme weight that it does not mix with liquids? Indeed, all liquids float on top of it, meaning it must naturally sink to the bottom.

Eighth: why does **Quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: Known in Latin as *Argentum Vivum*, this is the liquid metal Mercury, named for its "living" or "mobile" appearance.</term> "embody" itself, so to speak, and unite with all metals? It is joined with **Gold** in a particularly marvelous union, as no one can easily separate it from any metal or body with which it has been mixed.

Mercury
</margin>

Therefore, those who wish to avoid the damage caused by quicksilver—such as **gilders** <term>gilders: Artisans who traditionally used mercury amalgams to apply gold to objects, a process that often led to mercury poisoning through toxic fumes.</term> and those suffering from the **French disease** <note>original: "morbo Galico." For this reason, if quicksilver has been put into someone's ear, an immediate remedy is to place a small ball of Gold in the ear. The **Mercury** is immediately drawn out and merges with the golden ball. We recently saved a man this way, whose wicked and unfaithful wife had secretly poured quicksilver into his ear; he certainly would have died if we had not assisted him in this way.

<meta>This page continues the scientific and philosophical inquiry into the nature of Mercury (Quicksilver). While the previous page discussed its physical weight and its medicinal/gilding uses, this section shifts to its internal composition and the possibility of solidifying it.</meta>

Ninth: why is **Quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver: The liquid metal mercury, known for its silver color and "living" or fluid movement.</term> white and bright, and why is it heavy? Is it perhaps because of its watery and earthy parts, as we have stated above? For its body is **heterogeneous** <term>heterogeneous: A substance made of different parts or elements that are not uniform throughout.</term>.

<margin>
This is false, for Mercury does not have in itself any heterogeneity in its composition, except for that which is accidental and very easily separated, as the **Philosophers** <note>In this context, "Philosophers" usually refers to alchemists or natural philosophers who sought the "Philosophical Mercury," a purified version of the metal.</note> know; and then it is their Mercury.
</margin>

Tenth: can Quicksilver be **frozen**? <term>frozen: In the Latin *congelatur*, this refers to "coagulation," or the process of turning the liquid metal into a solid.</term> It seems, according to **Aristotle** <note>Aristotle (384–322 BCE) was the primary authority for medieval and Renaissance science; his *Meteorology* discussed the formation of minerals.</note> in the second book of *Meteorology*, chapter 43, that it can be inferred that it cannot be frozen or coagulated. For it appears, according to Aristotle, to contain more air than water, and therefore it does not freeze from the cold. For this reason, **Buccaferrus** <note>Lodovico Boccadiferro (1482–1545) was a celebrated Italian professor of philosophy at Bologna who provided influential commentaries on Aristotle's works.</note> used to exclaim to his disciples during his lectures on the fourth book of *Meteorology*: "Gentlemen, do not believe those **chemists** <note>original: "chimistis." Here, the author likely refers to alchemists who claimed to be able to "fix" or solidify mercury.</note>, for they lose what they have in order to acquire what they do not have," alluding to the idea that Quicksilver cannot be coagulated. 

I respond that Quicksilver is not coagulated by itself or by cold, but rather by the mixing of another substance, as **Geber** <note>Geber is the Latinized name of Jabir ibn Hayyan (c. 721–815), a foundational figure in the history of chemistry and alchemy.</note> and the chemists do. It is coagulated from these substances, and according to **Albertus** <note>Albertus Magnus (c. 1200–1280) was a scholar and saint whose writings on minerals were central to medieval science.</note>, it can be done with sulfur—provided the sulfur does not burn it entirely—or by the insertion of green wood. Specifically, he refers to the tree he calls the **hazel** <note>original: "corylum... Auellanam." Both terms refer to the common hazel tree (*Corylus avellana*).</note>; for when this wood is dried by heat, it draws out the humidity of the Quicksilver. Buccaferrus read in Albertus of another method, namely that Quicksilver is coagulated by a stick...

**Mercury** <note>original: *curius*, completing the word *Mercurius* from the previous page.</note>, for which reason in cases of **Ileus** <term>Ileus: A painful and life-threatening obstruction of the bowel where the digestive tract stops moving.</term>, when the situation is desperate and other remedies have not helped, it should be administered without hesitation or delay, as we have stated in our account of **Volvulus** <term>Volvulus: A condition where a loop of intestine twists around itself and the mesentery that supports it, causing a bowel obstruction.</term>. **Avicenna** <note>original: *Avic.* (Ibn Sina, c. 980–1037), a Persian polymath whose "Canon of Medicine" remained a standard medical text in Europe for centuries.</note>, in Book 4, Treatise 1, Section 6, Chapter 2, stated that for many people it was not harmful when it passed through the bowels.

The liquid, metallic form of mercury.</note>. **Forestus** <note>Pieter van Foreest (1521–1597), a Dutch physician known for his extensive collection of medical case observations.</note> notes the same. **Marianus Sanctus** <note>Marianus Sanctus (1490–1550), an Italian surgeon famous for his work on the removal of kidney stones.</note> tells a memorable story of a certain woman who, due to various afflictions, frequently and repeatedly took a **half-pound** <note>original: *lib. β*. The Greek letter beta was commonly used in medical manuscripts to denote "one half."</note> of Quicksilver and expelled it through the bowels without harm.

Most recently of all, in his letters, **Horatius Augenius** <note>Orazio Augenio (1527–1603) was an Italian professor of medicine at Turin and Padua.</note> recommends to **Celius** that this Quicksilver should be used not "extinguished" <term>extinguished: A process where mercury was rubbed with saliva, fats, or other substances until the metallic globules were no longer visible. Augenius recommends using the pure liquid metal instead.</term>, but first washed in very sharp vinegar and contained in a thin membrane. He suggests it for the "Great Disease" <note>original: *morbo magno*. It aids all these conditions by reason of its **heat** and **dryness**; and in the case of worms, it removes their nourishment by reason of its dryness. ...Dismiss those things which <note>The text cuts off here, continuing on the next page.</note>

**Fallopius** <note>Gabriele Falloppio (1523–1562), one of the most important anatomists and physicians of the 16th century.</note>, etc. [writes]: "I know that **Antonius Gallus** <note>Antonio Gallo, a physician known for his works on the French Disease.</note> and others list it among **Poisons** <note>original Latin: *Venena*</note>, but if it ever causes harm, it is perhaps by reason of its weight, as shall be discussed below. Therefore, it is not a Poison."

And although this author says even more, this is what seemed relevant for my purpose: to make it known that **Mercury** is not the poison that many believe it to be. Instead, it is the greatest remedy that can be found in the world for expelling every kind of disease that cannot be eradicated by means of other remedies—specifically the **French Disease** <term>French Disease: Historically known as *morbo Gallico*, this was the common term for syphilis in the early modern period.</term>, even if it is long-standing <note>original Italian: *inueterato* (inveterate).</note>. Furthermore, for **worms**, it is so miraculous that there is no equal in the world; and in **Catalonia**, women give it raw in this manner to their children for that purpose,

Mercury is not a Poison, but rather an excellent remedy for many infirmities.

<meta>This page continues the long quotation from Saint Augustine's *City of God* (Book 11, Chapter 22) that began on page 70. The author is using Augustine’s theological authority to support the idea that all things in nature, even those that seem harmful, have a divinely ordained purpose.</meta>

[...at first,] they offend; such as fire, or cold, or a wild beast, or something of this sort. Nor do they observe how well they flourish in their own places and natures, and are arranged in a beautiful order; and how much beauty they contribute to the whole universe in their own proportions, as if to a common commonwealth <note>original: "comunem rempublicam". Augustine compares the order of the universe to a well-governed city or republic.</note>, or how much convenience they might grant to us ourselves if we use them appropriately and skillfully. Thus, even **Poisons** <note>original: "Venena"</note> themselves, which are harmful through improper use, are turned into **Wholesome medicines** <note>original: "Salubria medicamenta"</note> when applied appropriately; just as, on the contrary, even those things in which we take pleasure—such as food and drink and this light—are felt to be harmful through immoderate and untimely use.

Whence **Divine Providence** <note>original: "diuina prouidentia"</note> warns us not to foolishly disparage things, but to diligently inquire into the **utility of things** <note>original: "utilitatem rerum"</note>; and where our intellect or weakness fails, to believe in those things hidden from us, just as there were some things we could hardly find [in the past]. For even that hiding of utility is either an exercise in humility or a wearing down of pride, since absolutely no nature is evil <note>original: "natura nulla sit malū"</note>, and this name <term>Evil</term> <note>Augustine defines evil not as a physical substance, but as the absence or corruption of a natural good.</note> is nothing except the **privation of good** <note>original: "priuationis boni"</note>. And what follows.

<meta>This page continues the argument following the quotation from Saint Augustine on the previous page. [Natu]ra <note>The text begins with the suffix "-ra," completing the word "Natura" (Nature) from the previous page.</note> having provided us with everything that we need, both for sustenance as well as for the preservation of health and the curing of **diseases** <note>original: "morbi"</note>, she removes the need to sail the seas <note>The OCR reads "solcar le mani" (to furrow the hands), but the context of international trade suggests the author intended "solcar i mari" (to sail the seas).</note> and to trade with barbarous nations for **drugs** <term>Droghe</term> <note>In the early modern period, "droghe" referred to imported medicinal spices, resins, and chemicals, which were often subject to high taxes and markups.</note> that cost very high prices. Instead, she gives to every climate everything that is needed for the health of Living Beings. 

And having placed so many virtues in these two subjects—that is, **Antimony** and **Mercury**—despite the little bit of evil that seems to us to be in them, we must not abandon them. For with very little expense and with the greatest ease, by their means, we can conquer every known disease. It is enough only to set oneself to the work and not remain buried in one's own opinions, nor give credit (as the common folk mostly do) to foreign things more than to those we have, and which Mother **Nature** has provided for our needs just as she has in other places. ->TO THE READER.<-

We have understood in the first book what the origin, nature, and properties of <term>Mercury<note>The element quicksilver, which was a cornerstone of both alchemy and Renaissance medicine, particularly in the controversial treatment of diseases like syphilis.</note></term> are, where with the great authority of serious Ancient and modern Authors, we may recognize and consider its inestimable virtues, and from this judge whether its use should be abhorred; and it is indeed true that Mercury

<meta>This page continues from the previous page's discussion of Mercury, completing the sentence regarding its preparation for use.</meta>

Mercury, before using it in any way, must be <term>purified<note>The original term is 'purgatione.' In early modern chemistry and alchemy, this involved removing 'dross' or 'accidental impurities' from quicksilver through processes like distillation or washing with vinegar and salt.</note></term>, as it contains within itself many accidental impurities. Through this purification, it is <term>cleansed<note>Original: 'mondifica.' A term suggesting a process of making the substance 'neat' or refined for medicinal use.</note></term> and cleared of them. 

Here in this second Book, many ways of purifying it will be discussed according to the inventions of many <term>Authors<note>Refers to established medical or alchemical authorities, likely including figures such as Geber, Rhazes, or more contemporary Renaissance physicians.</note></term>. These will be explained in the <term>vernacular<note>Original: 'volgarmente.' By writing in the common tongue (Italian) rather than Latin, the author aims to make this specialized knowledge accessible to common practitioners and craftsmen.</note></term> so that they are intelligible to all. In this way, everyone will be able to choose the best method according to the <term>operation<note>In this context, an 'operation' refers to a specific chemical procedure or the preparation of a particular medicine.</note></term> they wish to perform. 

As long as the practitioner is experienced, they will find here in this Second Book the beginning of every good. May it please the Lord to enlighten them and lead them to the good path of <term>health<note>Original: 'salute.' In the 16th and 17th centuries, this word carried the double meaning of physical health and spiritual salvation.</note></term>, which God may grant to all in this life and the next. 

And so be it; stay healthy.

# Of the Selection of Mercury.

### Chapter I.

<margin>Mercury Mine in Slavonia.</margin>
Two species of Mercury are found, namely, natural and made or artificial. The natural is found in particular mines, specifically in <term>Slavonia<note>Historically referring to a region in the eastern Alps/Balkans; here it refers to the territory under the House of Austria.</note></term>, in the County of Gorizia, in a town called <term>Idria<note>Modern-day Idrija in Slovenia, home to the second-largest mercury mine in the world, which began operations in the late 15th century.</note></term>. This mine is abundant both in "running" mercury <note>Natural liquid mercury found in its pure state in the earth.</note> as well as within <term>Cinnabar<note>Mercury sulfide (HgS), the primary ore from which mercury is extracted.</note></term>, which they extract by force of fire—much like <term>Oil of Holy Wood<note>Original: 'Oglio di legno Santo.' This refers to Guaiacum wood, highly prized in the Renaissance for treating syphilis. Its oil was extracted through a similar heating process.</note></term> is made—by <term>descent<note>Original: 'per descenso.' A distillation method where the heat is applied from above or around a vessel, forcing the vapors or liquid to flow downwards into a receiver.</note></term>. It is found similarly and in the same form in Spain, in the Kingdom of Castile, near a place called <term>Almagro<note>The author likely refers to the Almadén district near Almagro, the world's most productive mercury mine since antiquity.</note></term>. This mine is not as abundant as the aforementioned one, but the Mercury is much purer and surpasses every other in quality. Of this Spanish mercury, we can obtain very little unless it is specially ordered; and yet, it would be necessary for those who wish to reach a successful conclusion of the <term>Great Work<note>Original: 'gran’ Opera.' The ultimate goal of alchemy: the creation of the Philosopher's Stone or the transmutation of base metals into gold.</note></term>.

<margin>Mercury Mine in Spain</margin>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Artificial Mercury" mentioned at the end of page 82. The first word "Artte" (Art) completes the phrase "sons of the Art," referring to alchemists or skilled practitioners of the chemical arts.</meta>

**Art**, and it is extracted from minerals and from metals by means of <term>resurrective salts<note>Original: 'Sali resuscitativi.' These are chemical agents—often salts like sal ammoniac or vitriol—used in alchemy to "revive" or release the essence of a metal from its mineral ore.</note></term>. But it so happens that when Mercury is not of the required quality, the <term>Operations<note>A general term for alchemical processes such as distillation, sublimation, or calcination.</note></term> often do not succeed; therefore, to test it, one shall take a spoon of pure **Silver** and put inside it a little of the Mercury which one wishes to test. And let it be placed to evaporate over glowing coals; and once it has evaporated, observe what stain it leaves inside the spoon. For if it leaves a black stain, its impurity comes from **Lead**; if cerulean <note>A sky-blue or blue-gray color.</note>, from **Tin**; if golden, from **Copper**. If the spoon remains white without any other color, it is of a golden nature; if reddish-yellow, it is **Argentine** <note>Original: 'Argentea.' Meaning silver-like or derived from silver.</note>; and thus one must choose that which leaves the yellow or white stain, which is the best of all. The others, if one wishes to use them, require an extraordinary diligence in <term>purging<note>To 'purge' is to purify the substance, removing the 'dross' or secondary metals that contaminate the mercury.</note></term> them. 

Although, as a matter of good precaution, whatever sort of Mercury it may be, it must be purged with every diligence; therefore, here I will set down several ways.

# First Purgation of Mercury.

### Chapter II.

Take some excellent <term>spirit of wine<note>Original: 'Spirito di Vino.' Highly distilled alcohol, used here as a solvent to wash away surface impurities from the metal.</note></term> inside a long-necked <term>flask<note>Original: 'bozza.' A glass laboratory vessel.</note></term>, and place the Mercury into it. Shake it thoroughly until you see that the spirit has turned black; at that point, pour off the aforementioned tinted spirit by <term>decanting<note>Original: 'per inclinatione.' Literally 'by inclination,' the process of tilting the vessel to pour off the liquid while leaving the heavier metal behind.</note></term> it. Pour fresh spirit of wine over the Mercury remaining inside the flask, shaking it as before; when it turns black, pour it off by decanting. Repeat this operation until the Mercury is found entirely in a state of blackness. This black substance shall be taken, and after it is dry, it shall be mixed with <term>quicklime<note>Original: 'calcina viva.' Calcium oxide, a caustic substance used in alchemy to 'fix' or help separate materials during heating.</note></term>. It shall then be placed in a <term>retort<note>Original: 'storta.' A glass vessel with a long, curving neck used for distilling chemicals.</note></term> to distill, with its neck placed inside a <term>receiver<note>Original: 'recipiente.' The vessel that catches the condensed liquid.</note></term> containing fresh water, and the Mercury will distill forth in a most pure state.

<term>Purgation</term>, <term>Mercury</term>, <term>Spirit of Wine</term>, <term>flask</term>, <term>quicklime</term>, <term>retort</term>, <term>distill</term>

->Pur-<-

<summary>This page outlines a specific alchemical process for purifying mercury by washing it with alcohol until it turns into a black powder, which is then distilled with quicklime to recover the pure metal.</summary>
<keywords>mercury purification, distillation, spirit of wine, quicklime, alchemical laboratory, decanting, retort</keywords>

# Second Purgation of Mercury.

### Chapter III.

Take as much <term>bole<note>Original: 'Bollo.' Likely Armenian bole, a silicate earth or clay used in alchemy as a base or to help distribute heat evenly during distillation.</note></term> as the amount of Mercury you wish to prepare, and grind said bole finely. Then, reduce it to the form of an ointment using good <term>clarified honey<note>Original: 'miele despumato.' Literally 'despumated' or skimmed honey, heated to remove impurities and wax.</note></term>, and place the <term>quicksilver<note>Original: 'argento vivo.' A common historical name for mercury, referring to its fluid, "living" movement.</note></term> into this mixture, <term>amalgamating<note>Original: 'amalgamando.' The process of mixing mercury with other substances to create a uniform paste.</note></term> everything together. Next, it shall be placed to distill in a large <term>retort<note>Original: 'storta.' A glass laboratory vessel with a long, downward-curving neck.</note></term> that has a high neck. Once distilled, the Mercury shall be returned again to be mixed with new bole and new honey, as described above, repeating this until the third time. You will then have a most excellently purified Mercury, which will be of a celestial color and very bright.

# Third Purgation of Mercury.

### Chapter IV.

Liquid <term>black soap<note>Original: 'sapon nero.' Also known as corrosive sublimate or mercuric chloride, a highly toxic chemical salt used in advanced alchemical recipes.</note></term>, one and a half pounds.

## Fourth Purgation

### Chapter V.

<margin>

Take six ounces of the <term>Star Regulus of Mars<note>Original: 'Regolo di Marte stellato.' This is metallic antimony that has been refined using iron (Mars). A common method for purifying gold was to melt it with antimony, which would consume any silver or copper mixed with the gold.</note></term>. Amalgamate all of these together according to the Art, then pulverize the mixture. Combine this with one pound of excellent crystalline <term>sublimate<note>Original: 'Solimato.' Almost certainly mercuric chloride (corrosive sublimate), used here to react with the metals.</note></term> that has been powdered. Place this into a retort to distill according to the Art, and there will emerge a <term>butter<note>Original: 'butiro.' At the end, by increasing the heat of the fire, a great quantity of quicksilver will emerge, which will separate from the aforementioned butter; this Mercury will precipitate into the water and [be washed]...

The reason why Mercury should be given both with purgatives and in pills.

expelling them out of the body except weakly; thus, with a little bit of a stimulus <note>original: *sprono*; literally a "spur," referring to the purgative acting as a catalyst to help the mercury move the humors.</note> it then performed its function excellently. Furthermore, it will always be better to administer it in **Pills**, because in this way it is swallowed and goes directly to the stomach without being able to bring any harm to the joints or to the mouth, etc. For when taking it with **Electuaries** <term>eletuarij; medicinal pastes made by mixing powders with honey or syrup</term>, or preserves, or in a drink, while drinking or chewing it, it can get stuck between the gums and ruin them along with the teeth, and also cause a flow from the mouth <note>original: *flusso di bocca*; a reference to "salivation," a common and often intentional but dangerous side effect of mercury treatment in the Renaissance.</note>; therefore, everyone should be careful of this.

Now, the aforementioned preparation is an Excellent remedy for all infirmities caused by putrefaction, and for jaundice, **Dropsy** <term>Idropisia; an old term for edema or swelling caused by excess fluid in the body</term>, skin diseases, daily fevers, and others.

# Sixth Precipitate.

### Chapter XII.

Take some **Sublimated Mercury** <term>Mercurio solimato; corrosive sublimate or mercury(II) chloride, a highly toxic white salt used as a base for many chemical medicines</term>, and let it be dissolved in water by boiling. Once dissolved, pour over the said solution **Oil of Tartar** <term>Oglio di Tartaro; a concentrated liquid form of potassium carbonate produced by "deliquium," where the salt is left to absorb moisture from the air until it liquefies</term>, and do this drop by drop. You will see the **Mercury** precipitate into a red powder. When it has finished precipitating, pour off the liquid by tilting the vessel. Then, wash it several times with spring water to remove all harshness and saltiness from it. Finally, wash it two or three times with some **cordial water** <term>acque cordiale; medicinal waters, often distilled from herbs like borage or balm, intended to strengthen the heart and vital spirits</term>, and afterwards let it be dried and stored for use as follows.

Given with **Theriac** <term>Theriaca; a legendary, complex multi-ingredient paste used as a universal antidote and panacea</term> or **Mithridate** <term>Mitridato; an ancient semi-solid medicine similar to Theriac, originally formulated as an antidote for King Mithridates VI</term> in the morning on an empty stomach several times, according to the illness and the need of the patient. Taking this every three days marvelously opens all obstructions, especially in cases of **jaundice** <note>original: *giallezza*</note>, and the **pale colors** <note>This likely refers to "green sickness" or chlorosis, a form of anemia historically associated with a pale or greenish complexion.</note> that occur, and similar conditions. It is also uniquely effective against any **Poison** whatsoever, and in **putrid fevers** <term>febbri putride; a historical classification for severe, often infectious fevers—such as typhus or malaria—thought to be caused by the "putrefaction" of internal humors</term>, and...

# Ninth Precipitate, or Mineral Mummy, of the Most Excellent Pierre Potier, of Angers <note>original: "Pietro Potterio, Andegauenſe"; Pierre Potier (1581–1640) was a famous French physician and alchemist known for his chemical medicines.</note>.

This must be done with great diligence until the water that is added is poured off as sweet and tasteless as it was when it was put in. Then, wash it two or three times with some cordial water <term>acqua cordiale; a medicinal water intended to stimulate the heart and spirits</term>, and if desired...

## Eleventh Precipitate.

# Twelfth Green Precipitate.

### Chapter XVIII.

Let three ounces of **purified mercury** be dissolved in as much <term>common nitric acid; historically known as aqua forte</term> as is sufficient, and separately, half an ounce of **pure copper** shall also be dissolved in the same acid. Then, combine these solutions inside a <term>retort; a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck</term> and distill the nitric acid over a **sand bath** <note>a method of heating a vessel evenly by nesting it in a container of sand</note>, increasing the fire at the end to drive out the spirits of the nitric acid. Then, having allowed the retort to cool, let it be broken and the precipitate taken and reduced to a very fine powder. Place this inside a glass vessel with **distilled vinegar** and let it digest <note>a process of slow, gentle heating</note> for twenty-four hours in a **warm bath**, and afterwards let it boil so that a large part of the mercury is dissolved in the said vinegar. This [liquid] shall be poured off by decanting <note>original: "per inclinatione"; tilting the vessel to pour off the liquid without disturbing the sediment</note> once it has become colored, and more vinegar should be added, digesting it as before and then boiling it in the same manner

# The Thirteenth Admirable Precipitate

### Chapter XIX

Take one pound of purified Mercury and dissolve it in <term>aqua regia; <note>original: "acqua regia"</note> a highly corrosive mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids, so named because it can dissolve "noble" metals like gold</term>. Make a Precipitate according to the Art <note>referring to the standard chemical procedures of the period</note>, which shall be dissolved in distilled vinegar through several extractions. These shall be filtered, and the vinegar shall be extracted [evaporated] in a <term>bath; <note>original: "bagno"</note> a water bath, often called a 'balneum Mariae,' used for gentle and even heating</term>. 

Upon the residue remaining in the <term>flask; <note>original: "bocia"</note></term>, add fresh vinegar again; after separating it from the <term>dregs; <note>original: "feccie"</note> the solid sediment or impurities</term>, the vinegar is again to be separated [evaporated] as before. Continue adding new vinegar to the remainder, extracting and separating, as many times as necessary until the Mercury dissolves entirely without leaving any dregs, and until it remains like an ointment or Salt after the vinegar has been extracted. 

Now, take half of the aforementioned substance and pour over it some <term>Spirit of Wine; <note>original: "Spirito di Vino"</note> high-proof alcohol or ethanol</term>, so that the liquid floats two or three fingers deep above the substance. Seal the mouth of the flask very well and place it in <term>digestion; <note>original: "digeftione"</note> a process of slow, consistent heating to encourage chemical reaction</term> and <term>circulation; <note>original: "circolatione"</note> a process where vapors are condensed and flow back into the original liquid, often using a specialized vessel</term> for eight or more days. Afterward, place it in a <term>retort; <note>original: "ftorta"</note> a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids</term>,

...and by attaching a large receiver to them and sealing the joints very well, let it be distilled with graded heat. First, the <term>Spirit of Wine; <note>original: "Spirito divino," likely a variant or scribal error for "Spirito di vino"</note></term> and that of Mercury will come out, leaving the useless <term>caput mortuum; <note>original: "capo morto"; literally "dead head," the solid residue remaining after distillation</note></term> at the bottom of the <term>retort; <note>original: "storta"; a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling liquids</note></term>. One must take care, however, not to move the receiver from the retort at all until twenty-four hours have passed, because the Spirits do not precipitate easily, nor do they resolve into liquid Spirit. 

Once the said time has finished, place the whole mixture to distill in a <term>bath; <note>original: "bagno"; a water bath used for gentle, indirect heating</note></term> to separate the Spirit of Wine; the Oil—that is, the essence of Mercury, ash-gray in color—will remain in the flask. Afterward, place the said oil in a <term>fagiolo; <note>literally "bean"; a small, oval-shaped glass vessel used for digestion</note></term> with the other part of the Salt, and seal the mouth very well, placing it in the <term>heat of putrefaction; <note>original: "fuoco di putrefatione"; a low, steady heat intended to break down a substance, similar to the heat of organic decay</note></term> in an ash bath for two continuous months. Afterward, place it in a sand heat until the whole is converted into powder; then the fire shall be increased to a very strong <term>fixation; <note>original: "fissatione"; the process of making a volatile substance stable under heat so it no longer evaporates</note></term> heat until it is fixed, and it will be finished.

<margin>
The Spirit of Wine should be alkalized with the <term>tartar; <note>potassium bitartrate, a salty crust found in wine barrels, used here to adjust the chemical balance of the alcohol</note></term> that remains after the extraction of the Spirit.
</margin>

<margin>
If it is made as it should be, it will be a ruby or red color.
</margin>

The dose of this <term>Arcanum; <note>original: "Arcano"; a secret or powerful medicinal remedy</note></term> is from one grain to two, taken in wine or another appropriate liquor.
