# The Fifteenth Precipitate

### Chapter XXI

Take purified Mercury and the finest, pure Copper in equal parts. Let each be dissolved separately in <term>aqua fortis; <note>Latin for "strong water"; a highly corrosive nitric acid used to dissolve metals</note></term> made from <term>niter; <note>also known as saltpeter, a key ingredient in nitric acid</note></term> and <term>vitriol; <note>a sulfate of iron or copper</note></term>. Then, join the two solutions together and distill off the aqua fortis. The resulting precipitate should be washed several times to <term>sweeten; <note>original: "dolcificarlo"; in alchemy, this refers to washing a substance with water to remove corrosive salts or acids</note></term> it, and once dry, it should be preserved.

The dose is from two <term>grains; <note>a traditional unit of weight, roughly 65 milligrams</note></term> up to eight, or even more; it purges wonderfully without causing any nausea.

The said precipitate wonderfully purges the brain; therefore, it is excellent for any "fluxes" of the head resulting from <term>catarrh; <note>excessive buildup of mucus in the airways</note></term> or from the <term>French disease; <note>original: "causa gallica"; a common historical term for syphilis</note></term>. It is also excellent for <term>gout; <note>a painful form of inflammatory arthritis</note></term> and other joint pains when used alongside appropriate pills.

# Fifteenth Precipitate.

### Chapter XXI.

Take purified mercury and very fine and pure copper in equal parts; let each be dissolved separately in <term>strong water <note>original: "acqua forte"; a corrosive acid, typically nitric acid, used by early chemists to dissolve metals</note></term> made of <term>saltpeter <note>original: "nitro"; potassium nitrate</note></term> and <term>vitriol <note>a metal sulfate used here to create the acidic reaction needed to dissolve the copper and mercury</note></term>. Then, join the two solutions together and distill the strong water off of them; let the <term>precipitate <note>the solid substance that remains after the liquid has been evaporated or chemically separated</note></term> be washed several times to <term>sweeten <note>original: "dolcificarlo"; the process of rinsing a substance with water to remove any lingering corrosive acids or salts</note></term> it, and once dry, let it be preserved.

The dose is from two <term>grains <note>a traditional unit of weight, roughly 65 milligrams</note></term> up to eight, and even more, and it purges wonderfully without any nausea.

The said precipitate wonderfully purges the brain; therefore, it is excellent for any <term>fluxes <note>original: "flussioni"; an old medical term for an abnormal discharge of fluids, such as mucus, from the head</note></term> of the head proceeding from <term>catarrh <note>original: "cattari"; a buildup of mucus in the nose or throat</note></term> or from the <term>French disease <note>original: "causa gallica"; a common historical euphemism for syphilis</note></term>. It is also excellent for <term>gout <note>original: "gotte"; a form of inflammatory arthritis</note></term> and other joint pains when used with the appropriate pills.

Now take the said <term>Spirit <note>original: "Spirito"; in this context, the volatile liquid or vapor collected from the previous distillation.</note></term>, and place it inside a flask in a <term>water bath <note>original: "bagno"; specifically the "balneum mariae," a gentle heating method using a container of water to ensure even temperature.</note></term>. Place it in the <term>sand bath <note>original: "arena"; a method of applying higher heat by nesting the vessel in a tray of hot sand.</note></term> and distill it into a large receiver. Thus one will have the true <term>Oil of Salt <note>original: "Oglio di Sale"; historically referring to concentrated hydrochloric acid.</note></term>, which dissolves every body into <term>prime matter <note>original: "prima materia"; the fundamental substance from which all things are made, according to alchemical theory.</note></term>. However, it does not dissolve like aqua fortis, which does nothing but dismember the body; rather, this Oil <term>spiritualizes <note>original: "spiritualiza"; the process of converting a solid, "corporeal" substance into a purified, volatile form.</note></term> it, transforming it from a corporeal and fixed state to a spiritual and volatile one, in such a way that it can be given as <term>medicine <note>original: "medicnia" (medicina).</note></term>.

Now, to make the aforementioned <term>Oil of Gold <note>original: "Oglio d'oro"; a liquid form of gold, also known as "potable gold," believed to have profound healing properties.</note></term>, take three and a half ounces of Oil of Salt and one ounce of <term>calcined gold <note>original: "oro calcinato"; gold reduced to a fine powder through heat or chemical corrosion.</note></term>. Place it in a <term>testing flask <note>original: "sagiolo" (saggiuolo); a small glass vessel used for chemical trials.</note></term> under continuous heat so that it dissolves; afterwards, distill it through a sand bath, and the phlegm will be extracted.

# TRIUMPH
### O F
## MERCURY <note>In this period, "Mercury" often refers to both the Roman god of commerce and medicine, and the chemical element quicksilver, which was widely studied for its medicinal—and often toxic—properties.</note>
### Book three.

---

-> TO THE READER. <-

Never was there, nor will there ever be, anything more desired in the World <note>original: "Mōdo," an abbreviation using a tilde for "Mondo"</note> than health <term>sanità: physical well-being or soundness of body</term>; let a Man be as rich, powerful, Virtuous, honored, and revered by all as he may wish, yet if he does not enjoy the great and inestimable treasure of perfect health, he cares not for riches; he prizes not power; he enjoys not virtue, and he applauds neither light <note>original: "alumme," likely "al lume," referring to the light of knowledge or fame</note> nor honor

# THE TRIUMPH
### O F
## MERCURY
### Book Three.

-> TO THE READER. <-

There never was, nor will there ever be, anything more desired in the World than health <note>The author uses "sanità," referring to the physical state of being healthy.</note>, no matter how rich, powerful, Virtuous, honored, and revered by all a Man may be; for if he does not enjoy that great and inestimable treasure of perfect health <note>The author here uses "salute," which can imply both physical health and general well-being or salvation.</note>, he cares nothing for riches; he does not value power; he finds no joy in virtue; and he offers no praise to light or honor.

nor reverence from anyone; he would indeed give all the Gold of the World (if it were in his power) to buy a single drachma <note>A "dramma" or drachma was a very small unit of measurement; the author suggests even the smallest bit of health is worth more than all the world's wealth.</note> of perfect health; he would change his noble state for the most vile and abject, provided he could enjoy that so greatly desired Health; because, as **Plato** said in the ninth [book] of his Republic: **Among human goods, health is the foremost.** <note>original: "Inter bona humana primum esse sanitatem."</note> This was confirmed by **Stobaeus** <note>Joannes Stobaeus, a 5th-century scholar known for compiling extracts from Greek authors.</note> saying: **Health is surely the best thing for a mortal man.** <note>original: "Optima quidem est sanitas Viro mortali."</note> And **Orpheus**: **He who is healthy is divine.** <note>original: "Qui sanus est, divus est."</note>

*what then is there that a man would not do, when he is deprived of it, to reach its enjoyment? # ON THE SUBLIMATION
## O F
# MERCURY

### First Common Sublimate

->Chapter I.<-

Take four ounces of Roman Vitriol <note>A sulfate of copper or iron, essential in alchemical processes for its reactive properties.</note> and Saltpeter <note>Potassium nitrate, used as an oxidizing agent.</note>, and one pound each <note>original: "ana," a term used in prescriptions to indicate equal parts of the following ingredients.</note> of common Salt and Mercury. Incorporate everything together and unify it with a little vinegar. Then, place it in an appropriate flask <note>original: "bocia," a rounded distillation vessel.</note> to sublimate with its cap <note>original: "capello," the head of the alembic where vapors condense.</note> and receiver, applying graduated heat until all the water has exited. Afterward, increase the fire until you see that everything has sublimated. To make it purer and more crystalline, sublimate it again with one pound of Salt and four ounces of Vitriol. The third time, sublimate it with Salt alone; take care that the Salt must be decrepitated <note>Heated until it stops crackling to remove moisture trapped within the crystals.</note> and the Vitriol dried <note>original: "essicato."</note>.

<vocab>Sublimation, Mercury, Roman Vitriol, Saltpeter, Common salt, vinegar, flask, cap, receiver, decrepitated, dried</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the first chapter on the sublimation of mercury, providing a specific recipe involving vitriol, saltpeter, and salt, along with instructions for repeated refinement.</summary>
<keywords>sublimation, mercury, Roman vitriol, saltpeter, alchemical laboratory equipment, chemical refinement</keywords>

## Second Sublimate.

->Chapter II.<-

Take one pound of Mercury, four ounces of Vitriol <note>A metallic sulfate, likely iron or copper sulfate.</note>, four ounces of Sal ammoniac <note>Ammonium chloride, used in alchemy to aid the volatility and purification of metals.</note>, and four ounces of Rock salt <note>original: "Sal gema," naturally occurring crystalline sodium chloride.</note>. Moisten the whole mixture with vinegar and mix it together inside a marble mortar. Afterward, place it in a flask <note>original: "bocia," a round-bottomed glass vessel.</note> with its cap <note>The condensing head of an alembic.</note> and receiver. Once the vinegar has been distilled off, seal the beak of the cap very tightly so that no air may enter. Then, sublimate the Mercury by increasing the fire; you will obtain a sublimate as clear as crystal.

## Third Sublimate.

->Chapter III.<-

Calcine or dissolve the Mercury in aqua forte <term>literally "strong water"; a corrosive acid, typically nitric acid, used to dissolve metals.</term> (made from one part nitre and two parts Vitriol). Afterward, distill the water and take the Precipitate <note>The solid substance that remains or settles after the chemical reaction and distillation.</note>, and by mixing it with as mu— <note>The text breaks mid-word "tan-" (likely "tanto," meaning "so much" or "an equal amount"), to be continued on the following page.</note>

<vocab>Sublimate, Mercury, Vitriol, Sal ammoniac, Rock salt, vinegar, flask, cap, receiver, aqua forte, nitre, Precipitate</vocab>

<summary>This page details the second and third methods for sublimating mercury, utilizing various salts and acids like sal ammoniac and aqua forte to achieve a crystalline purity.</summary>
<keywords>sublimation, mercury, sal ammoniac, rock salt, aqua forte, nitric acid, alchemical distillation, laboratory practice</keywords>

...to Vitriol, calcined <note>original: "eficato," likely a variation of "essicato" (dried/calcined) or "rubificato" (reddened by heat).</note>, and common salt, decrepitated <note>Salt that has been heated until it stops crackling, indicating the removal of all moisture.</note>, of each as much as the weight of the Quicksilver. Place this in a flask to sublimate according to the Art <note>A phrase referring to the established standard practices of alchemy and chemistry.</note>, and afterward, mix it again with a quarter of its weight of salt as described above, and sublimate it for a second time. This is the best Sublimate there is for medicinal use.

## Fourth Sublimate with Regulus of Antimony

### Chapter IV.

Take purified Mercury and Regulus of Antimony <term>the metallic form of antimony, often considered more "noble" or pure after being separated from its ore.</term>, one pound of each; eight ounces of Rubified Vitriol <note>Vitriol (a metallic sulfate) that has been heated until it turns red.</note>; and one pound of decrepitated salt. Grind everything together and place it inside a testing jar <note>original: "Sagiolo," a small assaying vessel or crucible.</note>, pouring two or three ounces of aqua forte <term>literally "strong water"; nitric acid.</term> over it. Then sublimate according to the Art. Afterward, take the Sublimate and the dead head <term>original: "capo morto" (Latin: caput mortuum); the useless dregs or residue remaining after distillation or sublimation.</term>, and join them together once more; sublimate and repeat the said operation for a third time, and this...

## Second Sublimate.

->Chapter II.<-

Take one pound of Mercury <note>Commonly known as quicksilver.</note>, four ounces of Vitriol <note>A sulfate of a metal, likely iron or copper sulfate.</note>, four ounces of Sal ammoniac <note>Ammonium chloride; used as a flux in alchemy.</note>, and four ounces of Rock salt. <margin>Gerhard, et al. in the Tetras</margin> <note>original: "Ger- cet. in Tetr:" — likely referring to the *Tetras Chymiatrica* by the alchemist Johannes Gerhard</note> *That Mercury, however, is rendered philosophical in three ways: by sublimation, precipitation, and distillation.* <note>original: "Mercurius autem ille tribus modis redditur philoſophicus, ſublimatione, precipitatione, diſtillatione."</note> These three operations are found in the aforementioned [process]; and to better authenticate my opinion, let us hear what is said further on in the same place:

<meta>This page continues the technical explanation of the "essentialized Mercury." ...is given; and let it be called [r]us <note>original: "rus" — possibly a transcription error for "ros" (dew), a common alchemical term for the celestial substance that falls from the heavens</note> because it is able to recall Gold and Silver <note>original: "Solem, & Lunam," literally the Sun and the Moon</note> then into their first matter, and can return spiritual things from corporeal ones and living things from the dead, while preserving their radical moisture <term>the innate, life-sustaining fluid of a substance</term> and their seminal virtues, as well as the nature of fire and sulfur—secrets which the *Philosophers* <note>the term used for alchemists</note> have hidden for so long.

May the philosophers forgive me, however, if I strive to open up, for the sake of the scholarly world, that which has until now been veiled in wondrous obscurities, and to bring it with a clearer light to those noble, fiery spirits <note>original: "igneis"</note> and students of truth and secrets. This is what I also attempted just yesterday: I set it forth without enigmas, parables, or "blind" instructions, meant only for those seekers and not for malicious men who breathe nothing but envy, hatred, and ignorant arrogance, for whom I have no concern here.

I add, furthermore, that when this essentialized and exalted Mercury is dissolved in philosophical vinegar <term>a potent alchemical acid or solvent, not actual vinegar</term>, where Gold <note>original: "Sol"</note> has also been separately dissolved in a philosophical and proper proportion:

If both are joined and then separated from the solvent <term>original: "menstruo" — a term for a dissolving liquid, suggesting it acts like a life-cycle</term>, and then both are exalted through the art of sublimation <term>the process of vaporizing a solid and letting it collect again as a purified powder</term> by the segregation of moisture, then Mercury [is clothed] in the purple garment of Gold <note>original: "purpurea Solis veste"</note>...

**Key Terms Found in Text:**

*   **Gold and Silver** <gloss>original: "Solem, Lunam"</gloss>
*   **Radical moisture** <gloss>original: "radicalis humoris"</gloss> — the fundamental life-force in matter.
*   **Seminal [virtues]** <gloss>original: "Seminalium"</gloss> — the reproductive or generative power of a substance.
*   **Fire and Sulfur** <gloss>original: "ignis, sulphuris"</gloss> — two of the core elemental principles in alchemy.
*   **Philosophers** <gloss>original: "Philosophi"</gloss> — the ancient and medieval masters of alchemy.
*   **Secrets** <gloss>original: "arcanorum"</gloss> — the hidden mysteries of nature.
*   **Mercury** <gloss>original: "Mercurio"</gloss> — quicksilver, the primary material for the Great Work.
*   **Philosophical Vinegar** <gloss>original: "aceto philosophico"</gloss> — a specialized solvent.
*   **Gold** <gloss>original: "Sol"</gloss> — the "Sun" of the metals.
*   **Solvent** <gloss>original: "menstruo"</gloss> — the liquid that dissolves a solid.

## Ninth Lunar Sublimate

### Chapter IX.

Take six ounces of Regulus Sublimate <term>Regulus Sublimate: A chemical compound produced through sublimation, likely involving the "regulus" or pure metallic form of antimony combined with mercury.</term>, and two ounces of cupelled silver <term>Cupelled silver: High-purity silver that has been refined in a "cupel" to remove lead and other base metals.</term> filings or silver leaf. Let them be ground and joined together, and then set to sublimate over a sand bath <term>Sand bath: A vessel filled with sand and heated, used to provide a gentle, even temperature to glass equipment.</term>. Once the vessel has cooled, separate the volatile part from the fixed and crystalline parts, which will be found joined with the dregs. Return this to be sublimated again twice more as described above; afterwards, wash it several times and preserve it.

The dose is from six to ten grains <term>Grains: A traditional unit of weight in pharmacy, approximately 65 milligrams.</term>.

This is an excellent cephalic <term>Cephalic: A medicinal substance intended to treat ailments of the head, brain, or nervous system.</term> and it purges with gentleness.

# Common Cinnabar

### Chapter XIII.

Take one pound of sulfur <term>Sulfur: A yellow mineral essential to alchemy, often associated with the "combustible" or "soul" principle of matter.</term>, three pounds of quicksilver <term>Quicksilver: Metallic mercury, referred to here as "living mercury" (mercurio vivo).</term>; let the sulfur be melted inside an earthenware pot, and after it is melted, place the mercury inside a cloth, and by squeezing it little by little, let it fall into the melted sulfur, stirring very well with a small wooden stick so that the mercury incorporates perfectly with the sulfur. A black mixture <note>Historically known as "Ethiops mineral," this is the initial chemical reaction between mercury and sulfur before it is refined by heat.</note> will form, which shall be crushed, reducing it to powder, and placed in a flask very well sealed with luting <term>Luting: A sealant paste, usually made of clay, sand, and binders, used to make glass vessels airtight during heating.</term> with a cap that has a hole at the top for sublimating <term>Sublimating: The process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then collects again as a solid at the top of the vessel, used here to purify the cinnabar.</term>; leaving the hole in the cap open, give it a light, graduated fire at the beginning, increasing it little by little until a blackish-red smoke begins to be seen exiting, at which time give it a very violent fire for four or five hours, and it will sublimate the

Cinnabar <note>Cinnabar is the bright red ore of mercury (mercuric sulfide). In alchemy, it was prized both as a pigment and for its potent, though toxic, medicinal properties.</note> attaching itself to the sides of the vessel; once cooled, the vessel shall be broken, and the cinnabar removed, separating it from the dregs that will remain at the bottom.

Cinnabar can be administered orally as long as it is made with very pure sulfur and purified mercury; however, it is better to use flowers of sulfur <term>Flowers of sulfur: A very fine, purified powder produced by the sublimation of sulfur, considered more "noble" than raw brimstone.</term> instead of common sulfur. Its virtue is to purge through sweating <note>In early modern medicine, "diaphoretic" or sweat-inducing treatments were believed to expel "corrupt humors" or toxins from the body.</note>, and it is excellent for apoplexy <term>Apoplexy: Historically, a sudden loss of consciousness or paralysis, often corresponding to what we now call a stroke.</term>, epilepsy, and paralysis, and also for the French Disease <term>French Disease: A common historical name for syphilis (Morbo Gallico), often treated with mercury-based compounds.</term>, and other ailments where sweating is necessary.

Many preparations are made from cinnabar, and Milius <note>Refers to Johann Daniel Mylius (c. 1583–1642), a prominent German physician and alchemist whose works were widely cited for their practical chemical recipes.</note> teaches an excellent one for epilepsy, claiming to have performed a thousand experiments with it.

## Another Diaphoretic Cinnabar.

Take two ounces of Cinnabar and four drams of prepared Salt <note>Prepared salt usually refers to common sea salt that has been purified through dissolution and recrystallization.</note>; let them be reduced to powder and placed inside a small retort <term>Retort (stortino): A glass vessel with a long, curved neck used for distilling liquids.</term>, pouring over them three ounces of oil of sulfur made by the bell <term>Oil of sulfur made by the bell: A form of sulfuric acid produced by burning sulfur under a glass bell-shaped jar to collect the condensed vapors.</term>. ## Another Diaphoretic Cinnabar

### Chapter XVI

Sublimate <term>Sublimate: The chemical process of heating a solid substance until it turns into vapor and then allowing it to condense back into a solid form, usually to purify it.</term> the cinnabar <term>Cinnabar: A bright red mineral composed of mercury sulfide; while toxic by modern standards, it was a cornerstone of alchemical medicine when "purified."</term> with an equal part of sal ammoniac <term>Sal ammoniac: Ammonium chloride, a pungent mineral salt used in alchemy to help volatile substances rise during heating.</term>. The dose, the virtues, and the method of use are the same as described above.

# A Beautiful Preparation of Purgative and Diaphoretic Mercury.

### Chapter XVII.

<margin>How the solidification of mercury is performed</margin>
Let the mercury be solidified <term>Solidified (congelato): In historical alchemy, "freezing" or "congealing" did not refer to temperature, but to the process of turning liquid quicksilver into a solid state, usually by mixing it with another metal to form an amalgam.</term> with lead, which is done by melting the lead; when it has begun to cool, make a hole in the middle of it with a wooden stick. Afterward, place the quicksilver <term>Quicksilver (Mercurio vivo): Naturally occurring liquid mercury.</term> inside the aforementioned hole, and it will solidify immediately. In the same way, it may be solidified with tin when necessary.

Now, take one pound of the aforementioned mercury and grind it upon a painter’s stone <note>original: "pietra da Pitore"; a flat, hard slab—often made of marble or porphyry—used by artists and apothecaries to grind pigments or chemicals into an extremely fine powder.</note> until it is very well ground. Then, place it inside a glazed pot <term>Glazed pot (pignatta vitreata): A ceramic vessel with a smooth, glass-like coating on the inside to prevent the chemicals from soaking into the clay or reacting with the vessel itself.</term> with very strong vinegar mixed with salt, in such a way that the liquid rises two fingers’ breadth above the material. Leave it this way for three days, stirring it often with a wooden spatula. Then, let it boil until all the moisture is consumed <note>original: "consumatione di tutta l' humidità"; boiling the mixture until the liquid has completely evaporated, leaving only the treated solids behind.</note>, and afterward, pour in new vinegar

...and let it be boiled again as was said, and this is repeated for the third time; afterward, let it be taken, crushed, and washed several times with hot water. Then, place it inside a flask and pour distilled vinegar over it until it is covered, and let it sit in digestion <term>Digestion: the process of exposing a substance to gentle, continuous heat to extract properties or promote chemical change.</term> for twenty-four hours; then, distill it. This should be repeated three times, always using fresh vinegar. Afterward, take some Spirit of Wine <term>Spirit of Wine: Ethanol or high-proof alcohol, usually produced by distilling wine.</term> and distill it over the material for another three times, in the same manner as was described for the vinegar. 

Then, take the said mercury and let it dry very well inside an earthenware plate, and afterward, burn Spirit of Wine over it three times <note>This refers to pouring the alcohol over the substance and setting it alight to "purify" it through flame.</note>. Then, pulverize it again and place it in a bean-shaped vessel <note>original: "fagiolo"; likely a small, rounded distillation vessel or flask shaped like a bean.</note> with one and a half pounds of aqua forte <term>Aqua forte: Literally "strong water," the historical name for nitric acid.</term>, and it will be reduced to a very white calx <term>Calx (calcina): A powdery substance remaining after a metal or mineral has been thoroughly heated or reacted with acid.</term>. From this calx, the aqua forte is separated by decanting <note>original: "per inclinatione"; the process of pouring off the liquid from a vessel without disturbing the sediment at the bottom.</note>, and afterward, wash it several times with water, and finally two or three times with cordial water <note>original: "acqua cordiale"; a medicinal water, often infused with herbs or spirits, believed to strengthen the heart and act as a vehicle for the medicine.</note>. Let it dry and save a portion of it, which will serve as a purgative. Give a dose of four grains up to ten <note>A grain was a very small unit of weight, roughly equivalent to 0.06 grams.</note>, just like the Sublimate <term>Sublimate (Solimato): Specifically Corrosive Sublimate (Mercury(II) chloride), which was used in various chemical preparations despite its high toxicity.</term> of other preparations; afterward, drink a little Malvasia <note>original: "Maluasia"; Malmsey, a sweet, potent wine often used to help patients swallow or tolerate harsh medicines.</note> after taking it.

# An Excellent Remedy for Suckling Infants from one to six months old.

### Chapter XX.

Take one **ounce** of **flowers of sulfur** <term>Flowers of sulfur: A purified, bright yellow powdered form of sulfur used in historical medicine for its purported ability to "cleanse" the blood and skin.</term>, eight **scruples** <note>A scruple (scropolo) is an apothecary weight equal to about 1.3 grams.</note> of the aforementioned powder <note>Referring to the mercury and silver preparation described on page 162 for treating syphilis.</note>, prepared Oriental **pearls**, or half a **dram** <note>A dram (dramma) is an apothecary weight equal to about 3.9 grams.</note> of **magistery of pearls** <term>Magistery: A fine medicinal powder or precipitate, concentrated to enhance its healing properties.</term>, two scruples of **magistery of coral**, **white amber** <note>original: "Carabe bianco"; the term "Carabe" is an old name for amber derived from Arabic.</note>, **philosophically calcined stag's horn** <note>To calcine "philosophically" meant to reduce a substance to powder using controlled heat to preserve its perceived medicinal "spirit" rather than destroying it with high heat.</note>, and **ivory shavings**, one scruple of each <note>original: "anna"; a common medical abbreviation meaning equal parts of each.</note>, true **heart-bones of a stag** <term>Stag's heart bone: The *os de corde cervi*, a bone-like piece of cartilage found in the heart of a deer, highly prized as a heart tonic and remedy for "malignant" spirits.</term>, **Bezoar stone** <term>Bezoar stone: A solid mass found in the digestive systems of goats or deer, legendary for its power as a universal antidote to poison.</term>, and **gold leaf**, half a scruple of each, and six drams of **fine sugar**. Let it all be reduced to a very fine powder and preserved.

The dose is from six to ten **grains** <note>A grain (grano) is a tiny unit of pharmaceutical weight, approximately 0.05 grams.</note> in the **wet nurse's milk** <note>original: "latte della nutrice"; infants were often treated by adding medicine directly to the breast milk provided by their nurse.</note> for twenty or thirty days, giving to the said infants every three or four days some of the following **purgative powders** <term>Purgative: A medicinal substance used to cleanse the bowels and rid the body of harmful "humors."</term>; and this infallibly frees the said infants from every **malignancy** <note>original: "malignità"; in this context, it refers to the infectious nature of the disease or its harmful symptoms.</note>.

# Excellent pills for any itching or inflammation, and for every kind of skin lesion, particularly those resulting from the <term>French Disease</term><term>French Disease: A common historical name for syphilis (*Morbo Gallico*). Let the whole be mixed together.

The dose is ten grains every three days, taken in the evening before dinner.

These pills can be given to pregnant women and to children of approximately six or eight years of age with happy success.

And with this, I shall bring this third Book to an end.

This shall be poured back over the dregs <note>The solid remains in the bottom of the retort.</note> and distilled once more. In this way, a white, fragrant oil will emerge, free of any corruption, which drives out through **sweats** <term>Diaphoretic</term> <note>From the Greek *diaphorētikos*, meaning "promoting perspiration." ## Another fragrant oil of Mercury.

### Chapter III.

Take **Sal Ammoniac** <term>Sal Ammoniac</term> <note>Ammonium chloride, a mineral salt used by alchemists as a flux and for various chemical reactions.</note> and **lime** <term>lime</term> <note>Quicklime or calcium oxide, produced by heating limestone.</note> in equal parts; let both be placed, mixed together, inside a very well-covered **crucible** <term>crucible</term> <note>A ceramic or metal container used for melting substances at very high temperatures.</note>, to be **calcined** <term>calcined</term> <note>Heated to a high temperature to cause chemical change or purification.</note> over a most violent fire. Then, separate the salt with water by washing the said lime; once it is dry, return it again with more lime to be calcined as above, and let the said operation be repeated for a third time. ## Spirit or Red Martial Oil of Mercury.

### Chapter V.

When the distillation is finished, change the receiver to collect the phlegm, and after it is finished, increase the fire and collect the Oil as before. Then, repeat the aforementioned operation as many times as necessary until all of the aforementioned Oil or redness—which attaches itself to the flask and helm—is distilled into **oil, or Red Spirit**.

Over this oil, **Spirit of Wine** <note>A historical term for concentrated ethanol or high-proof alcohol.</note> shall be distilled several times to remove all the sharpness of the vinegar from it. In this way, one will have the **quintessence** or **Oil of Mercury**, as sweet as sugar and possessing virtues equal to **potable gold** <term>potable gold</term> <note>Original: *oro potabile*. A legendary alchemical elixir made of dissolved gold, believed to be a universal cure and a restorer of youth.</note> for every discoverable illness.

Its dosage is from two to six drops.

And its use is both external and internal.

# Ointment for pains of the head, and of joints, and for the tumors, and hardnesses caused by the **French Disease** <note>Original: *Morbo Galico*. A historical term for syphilis. In its secondary and tertiary stages, the disease caused severe bone and joint pain, as well as the formation of "gummas"—soft, tumor-like growths or hard swellings.</note>.

->Chapter XVII.<-

**Take** <note>Original Latin: *Recipe*. The standard imperative heading for medical prescriptions.</note> 3 ounces each of **Aragon** <term>Aragon Ointment</term> <note>A warming ointment typically containing rosemary, laurel, and other herbs, used to treat "cold" ailments of the nerves and joints.</note>, **Martiaton** <term>Martiaton Ointment</term> <note>Known as the "Soldier's Ointment," this was a complex compound of many herbs used to treat stiffness and aches.</note>, **Basilicum** <note>A classic ointment made of resin, wax, and oils, used to soothe and "ripen" swellings.</note>, and **Resin**. 1 ounce of **Pork Lard** <note>Original: *Asongia di porco*.</note>. 2 ounces each of **Laurel Oil**, **Dill Oil**, and **Iris Oil**. 1 ounce of **Oil of Spike** <note>Original: *Oglio di Spica*. An essential oil distilled from spike lavender (*Lavandula latifolia*), commonly used for joint pain.</note>, 6 ounces of **purified Quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>Mercury. This high concentration was the standard, albeit toxic, treatment for syphilis for centuries.</note>, 3 ounces of **Turpentine washed with Aqua Vitae** <note>Original: *termentina lauata con l'acqua vita*. Turpentine was "washed" with distilled spirits to purify it and make it more suitable for medicinal use.</note>, 1 ounce each of finely powdered **Chamomile flowers** and **Orris root powder** <note>Original: *radice d' Ireos*. The root of the Iris flower, valued for its scent and its ability to dry out "excess humors."</note>, 1 ounce of **Pine Resin**, and 1 ounce of **Wax**; let an ointment be made according to the Art <note>Original: *secondo l' Arte*. This instructed the apothecary to use their professional skill to properly emulsify the mercury into the fats and resins so it wouldn't separate.</note>.

## Ointment for those whose hair falls out because of the **French Disease** <note>Original: *morbo Gallico*. A historical name for syphilis. Patchy hair loss (alopecia) was a common and stigmatizing symptom of the disease's secondary stage.</note>.

->Chapter XVI.<-

**Take** <note>Original Latin: *Recipe*. The standard opening for a medical prescription.</note> 4 ounces of **Bear Grease** <note>Original: *Graffo d' Orso*. In early modern medicine, fats from strong animals like bears were thought to possess "strengthening" qualities that could stimulate hair growth.</note>, 3 ounces of **Oil of Southernwood** <term>Southernwood</term> <note>A woody herb (*Artemisia abrotanum*) in the wormwood family, traditionally used in remedies for baldness.</note>, 2 ounces of **Martiaton Ointment** <term>Martiaton</term> <note>Known as the "Soldier's Ointment," this was a complex compound of dozens of herbs used to treat stiffness and skin issues.</note>, 4 ounces of **prepared Quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>Mercury. This was the primary—though toxic—medical response to syphilis from the late 15th century until the early 20th century.</note>, 1 ounce of **Male Incense** <note>Also known as frankincense or olibanum; "male" refers to the highest quality, purest resin tears.</note>, half an ounce of **Myrrh**, three drams each of **Ash of Southernwood** and **Ash of Mole** <note>The use of animal ashes, particularly from burrowing animals like moles, was a common "sympathetic" remedy intended to help hair "break through" the skin.</note>, 3 ounces each of **Pine Resin** and **Turpentine washed in a decoction of Southernwood**, and 2 drams of **Wax**. Let an ointment be made according to the Art <note>A reference to the *Ars Pharmaceutica*, the professional standards and methods of the apothecary.</note>, and use it by lightly anointing the [affected] area.

# Ointment for headaches, joint pains, and for tumors and hardnesses caused by the **French Disease** <note>Original: *Morbo Galico*. A historical term for syphilis. In its secondary and tertiary stages, the disease caused severe joint pain and "hardnesses" or "tumors" known as gummas—soft, non-cancerous growths that could appear on the skin, bone, or internal organs.</note>.

### Chapter XVII.

**Take** <note>Original Latin: *Recipe*. The standard opening for a pharmaceutical prescription.</note> 3 ounces each of **Aragon Ointment** <term>Aragon</term> <note>A traditional warming salve often containing rosemary, laurel, and other herbs, used to treat "cold" ailments like joint stiffness.</note>, **Martiaton Ointment** <term>Martiaton</term> <note>Known as the "Soldier's Ointment," this was a complex preparation used to treat muscular pain and skin conditions.</note>, **Basilicon Ointment** <term>Basilicon</term> <note>A "royal" drawing ointment made of resins and fats, used to soften hard skin and draw out impurities.</note>, and **Resinous Ointment**. Add fresh **Hog's lard** <unclear>n.</unclear>, 2 ounces each of **Oil of Laurel**, **Oil of Dill**, and **Oil of Iris**. Include 1 ounce of **Oil of Spike** <term>Spike</term> <note>Oil derived from *Lavandula latifolia*, a type of lavender known for being more pungent and stimulating than common lavender.</note>, 6 ounces of **purified Quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>Mercury. This was the primary—albeit highly toxic—treatment for syphilis for centuries. It was believed to purge the "venereal venom" from the body, often through induced salivation.</note>, 3 ounces of **Turpentine washed with spirits of wine** <note>Original: *acqua vita*. Distilled alcohol used here to purify the turpentine resin.</note>, 1 ounce each of **Chamomile flowers** finely powdered and **Powder of Orris root** <note>Original: *radice d’ Ireos*. The root of the Iris plant, valued for its drying properties and pleasant scent.</note>, and 1 ounce each of **Pine Resin** and **Wax**. Let an ointment be made vigorously according to the Art <note>Original: *secondo l’ Arte*. This instruction tells the apothecary to follow the standard professional methods of preparation, specifically the laborious process of "killing" the mercury—mixing it so thoroughly with the fats that the metallic beads are no longer visible.</note>.

# Plaster for gummas and hard tumors caused by the **French Disease** <note>Original: *morbo Gallico*. In the 16th and 17th centuries, the later stages of syphilis were known for causing "gummas" (*gomme*), which are soft, non-cancerous growths that could appear on the skin or bones.</note>

->Chapter XVIII.<-

**Take** <note>Original Latin: *Recipe*. The standard opening for a pharmaceutical prescription.</note> 1 ½ ounces each of **Aragon Ointment** <term>Aragon</term> <note>A warming salve used for joint stiffness.</note>, **Martiaton Ointment** <term>Martiaton</term> <note>Known as the "Soldier's Ointment," used for muscle aches.</note>, **Basilicon Ointment** <term>Basilicon</term> <note>A "royal" drawing ointment used to soften hard skin.</note>, and **Resinous Ointment**. Include 1 ounce of each of the following: **Euphorbium** <term>Euphorbium</term> <note>A potent resin from the spurge plant used to irritate and stimulate the skin.</note>, **Ivy Gum**, **Gum Ammoniac**, **Opoponax**, and **Bdellium** <note>These are aromatic resins believed to have "resolving" properties, meaning they could break down hard tumors.</note>. Add 2 ounces of **Fir Turpentine**, half an ounce each of **Laurel Oil** and **Oil of White Lilies**, and 2 ounces of **quenched Quicksilver** <term>Quicksilver</term> <note>Mercury. "Quenching" or "killing" mercury involved mixing it with fat or saliva until the silver globules were no longer visible, allowing it to be absorbed by the skin.</note>. With as much **wax** as is necessary, let a plaster be formed.

# Another Plaster for the same purpose.

->Chapter XIX.<-

**Take** 2 ounces of **John of Vigo's frog plaster** <term>Frog Plaster</term> <note>A famous and expensive remedy created by the surgeon Giovanni da Vigo (c. 1450–1525). It contained mercury and was often prepared with the juices of frogs or earthworms.</note>. Add 1 ounce each of **Quicksilver**, **Euphorbium**, and **Orris root powder** <note>Original: *polvere d' Ireos*. The powdered root of the iris plant, used for its scent and drying properties.</note>. Form a plaster using **turpentine** washed with **brandy** <note>Original: *acqua vita*. Distilled spirits were used to "wash" or purify resins.</note>, and keep it until it is needed.

# Ointment for long-standing pains caused by the **French Disease** in the joints, and to induce sweating <note>Original: *morbo Gallico*. In the early modern period, syphilis was notorious for causing "bone-deep" pains. Doctors believed that "moving the sweats" helped the body expel the morbid humors (poisons) associated with the disease.</note>

->*Chapter XX:*<-

**Take** <note>Original Latin: *Recipe*. The traditional start of a medical recipe.</note> **Betony**, **White Horehound**, **Great Fleabane**, **Roman Wormwood**, **Calamint**, **French Lavender** <term>stecade arabico</term> <note>*Lavandula stoechas*, a Mediterranean lavender used for its warming and strengthening properties.</note>, **Rosemary**, **Marjoram**, and **Chamomile**, a handful of each. **Old Olive Oil**, 5 ounces. Let all the aforementioned herbs be boiled within the said oil with a pitcher of fragrant **white wine**, having first left them to steep <term>digestione</term> <note>The process of "digestion" in pharmacy involved soaking ingredients in liquid at a gentle heat to extract their medicinal virtues.</note> in the said oil and wine. Then, after everything has boiled for at least one hour, let it be pressed and strained. Return it to the fire to boil until all the moisture from the wine has been consumed <note>Meaning the wine has evaporated, leaving only the herb-infused oil.</note>. Afterward, add **turpentine** and **wax**, 1 pound and 3 ounces of each.

**Quicksilver** <term>Argento viuo</term> <note>Mercury. Let it be mixed and an ointment be made according to the art <note>Original: *secondo l'arte*. A standard pharmaceutical instruction meaning to follow professional standards of preparation.</note>.

# A Wonderful Plaster for Sores.

->Chapter XXVI.<-

Take one and a half ounces of **mercury** <note>Original: *Mercurio*. Also known as quicksilver.</note>, and two ounces of **lead**. Let the lead be melted, then create an **amalgam** <note>A mixture or alloy of mercury with another metal; in this case, lead.</note> with the aforementioned mercury. Let it be reduced to a fine powder and mixed with six ounces of **tutty ointment** <term>vnguento di tutia</term> <note>A traditional medicinal ointment made from zinc oxide, used for skin and eye ailments.</note>, two ounces of **new wax** <note>Fresh beeswax.</note>, and one ounce of **turpentine**. Form this into a plaster.

# An Excellent Powder for the Same Purpose.

->Chapter XXVII.<-

Take **Crocus Martis** <note>Literally "Saffron of Mars," a historical name for red iron oxide.</note>, **Crocus Veneris** <note>Literally "Saffron of Venus," a historical name for copper oxide.</note>, and **minium** <note>Red lead.</note>, four drams of each <note>Original: *anna*, from the Greek *ana*, meaning "of each."</note>; and one and a half drams of **calomel** <term>mercurio dolcificato</term> <note>Literally "sweetened mercury," a less corrosive form of mercury (mercurous chloride) used internally and externally.</note>. Let the whole mixture be reduced to a very fine powder and preserved.

# Silvering or Beauty Wash

### Chapter XXVIII.

Take one pound of <term>Sublimated Mercury<note>original: "Mercurio Solimato"; also known as corrosive sublimate or mercuric chloride, a highly toxic substance used historically in cosmetics for its skin-peeling and whitening effects.</note></term>, four ounces of Refined Borax, three ounces of <term>Quicksilver<note>original: "Argento viuo"; elemental mercury.</note></term>, and two ounces of <term>Litharge of Silver<note>original: "litargirio d' Argento"; a form of lead oxide produced during the separation of silver from lead ore.</note></term>. Make it into a very fine powder by grinding it on a marble slab. Then, place it inside a suitable glass vessel and pour water over it for eight days, changing the said water every day. Afterward, let it dry, and then grind it again upon a marble slab, mixing in one ounce of Camphor. Store it in a glass jar, very well-sealed. When you wish to use it, it should be mixed with some appropriate <term>floral water<note>Distilled herbal waters were the standard base for liquid cosmetics.</note></term>, such as broad bean flower water, or <term>water lily<note>original: "nenufari"</note></term> water, or pumpkin water, or another. If you enjoy the scent, you may mix in a little of any fragrant water as you please. Thus, with that water very well beaten with a little of the aforementioned powder, which will make it become

<summary>A recipe for a white cosmetic face wash (belletto) made from a toxic mixture of mercury, borax, and lead oxide, intended to be diluted with floral waters.</summary>
<keywords>cosmetics, mercury, borax, litharge, camphor, floral waters, skin whitening, Renaissance beauty</keywords>

## A Very Beautiful and Proven Cosmetic

### Chapter XXIX.

Take one dram of <term>Sublimated Mercury<note>original: "Solimato"; mercuric chloride, a toxic skin-whitening agent.</note></term>, two scruples of Oriental pearls, and two ounces of <term>Porcelain<note>original: "Porcelane"; likely referring to crushed cowrie shells or fine white ceramic, used for their pearlescent quality and abrasive texture.</note></term>. Grind everything together upon a <term>porphyry slab<note>A hard volcanic stone used for grinding ingredients into an extremely fine, "impalpable" powder.</note></term> until it is so fine it cannot be felt. Then, wash it very well to <term>sweeten it<note>original: "dolcificarlo"; the process of removing corrosive or salty impurities through repeated rinsing.</note></term> with <term>broad bean flower water</term>. Afterward, let it dry and keep it for use.

One should be warned, however, not to use this or any other cosmetic where <term>Sublimated Mercury</term> or mercury is present, unless the body is first clean and <term>purged<note>Renaissance medical theory suggested that applying heavy metals to the skin could be dangerous if the "humors" of the body were not first balanced or "purged" through fasting or laxatives, as the toxins might otherwise be driven deeper into the internal organs.</note></term>.

# Another Excellent Cosmetic

### Chapter XXX.

Take one dram of <term>sublimated mercury<note>original: "solimato"; mercuric chloride, a common though toxic whitening agent.</note></term>, two drams of <term>quicksilver<note>original: "Argento Viuo"; elemental mercury.</note></term> <term>extinguished<note>original: "estinto"; the process of "killing" mercury involved stirring it until the visible globules disappeared, often using saliva or fatty substances to create an emulsion.</note></term> with saliva, one dram each of Oriental pearls and <term>porcelain<note>original: "Porcelane"; refers to cowrie shells or fine white ceramic used to provide a pearlescent luster.</note></term>, and one and a half drams of camphor. Combine everything together inside a marble mortar with a wooden pestle, and then grind it upon a <term>porphyry stone<note>A slab of hard volcanic rock used by apothecaries to grind powders to an "impalpable" or microscopic fineness.</note></term>. Let it be washed as was described above <note>Referring to the process of "sweetening" or rinsing the mixture with floral water to remove corrosive salts.</note>, and once dry, preserve it for use.

Many, many other diverse operations are performed with Mercury, as can be seen in various authors; but he who desires more should seek out the aforementioned authors, where he will find every desired satisfaction, should he not be content with what I have placed in this book. It seems to me that I have written enough to make known the truth of the great virtue of Mercury, to the confusion of its <term>detractors<note>original: "maldicenti"; literally "those who speak ill." The author is likely defending mercury against the growing medical criticism of its toxicity during this period.</note></term>.

->THE END.<-

# Another Excellent Cosmetic

### Chapter XXX.

Take one dram of <term>sublimated mercury<note>original: "solimato"; mercuric chloride, a powerful but highly toxic chemical used in the 17th century to peel the skin and create a pale complexion.</note></term>, two drams of <term>quicksilver<note>original: "Argento Viuo"; elemental liquid mercury.</note></term> <term>extinguished<note>original: "estinto"; the process of "killing" mercury by grinding it with substances like saliva or fats until the metallic globules were no longer visible and it became an emulsion.</note></term> with saliva, one dram each of Oriental pearls and <term>porcelain<note>original: "Porcelane"; likely referring to crushed cowrie shells, which provided a white, pearlescent pigment for the skin.</note></term>, and one and a half drams of camphor. Combine everything together inside a marble mortar with a wooden pestle, and then grind it upon a <term>porphyry stone<note>A slab of very hard volcanic rock used by early modern chemists and apothecaries to grind ingredients into an extremely fine, "impalpable" powder.</note></term>. Let it be washed as was described above <note>This refers to "sweetening" the mixture by rinsing it with water or flower distillates to remove harsh acidic salts.</note>, and once dry, preserve it for use.

Many, many other diverse operations are performed with Mercury, as can be seen in various authors; but he who desires more should seek out the aforementioned authors, where he will find every desired satisfaction, should he not be content with what I have placed in this book. It seems to me that I have written enough to make known the truth of the great virtue of Mercury, to the confusion of its <term>detractors<note>original: "maldicenti"; literally "those who speak ill." The author is defending the medicinal and cosmetic use of mercury against growing contemporary concerns about its poisonous effects.</note></term>.

->THE END.<-
